Night-time summer temperatures range from the upper 50s °F (14°C) in the West Texas mountains to 80°F (27°C) in Galveston.[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-168)[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-169)

The table below consists of averages for August (generally the warmest month) and January (generally the coldest) in selected cities in various regions of the state.

| [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston "Houston") | 94/75 | 34/24 | 63/54 | 17/12 |
| [San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio "San Antonio") | 96/74 | 35/23 | 63/40 | 17/5 |
| [Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas "Dallas") | 96/77 | 36/25 | 57/37 | 16/3 |
| [Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin,_Texas "Austin, Texas") | 97/74 | 36/23 | 61/45 | 16/5 |
| [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso,_Texas "El Paso, Texas") | 92/67 | 33/21 | 57/32 | 14/0 |
| [Laredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laredo,_Texas "Laredo, Texas") | 100/77 | 37/25 | 67/46 | 19/7 |
| [Amarillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amarillo,_Texas "Amarillo, Texas") | 89/64 | 32/18 | 50/23 | 10/−4 |
| [Brownsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brownsville,_Texas "Brownsville, Texas") | 94/76 | 34/24 | 70/51 | 21/11 |

#### Storms

See also: [List of Texas hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_hurricanes "List of Texas hurricanes")

[Thunderstorms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thunderstorm "Thunderstorm") strike Texas often, especially the eastern and northern portions of the state. [Tornado Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado_Alley "Tornado Alley") covers the northern section of Texas. The state experiences the most [tornadoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado "Tornado") in the United States, an average of 139 a year. These strike most frequently in North Texas and the Panhandle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-171) Tornadoes in Texas generally occur in April, May, and June.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-172)

Some of the most destructive hurricanes in U.S. history have seriously impacted Texas. A hurricane in 1875 killed about 400 people in [Indianola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianola,_Texas "Indianola, Texas"), followed by [another hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1886_Indianola_hurricane "1886 Indianola hurricane") in 1886 that destroyed the town. These events meant that [Galveston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galveston,_Texas "Galveston, Texas") took over as the chief port city. The [1900 Galveston hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_Galveston_hurricane "1900 Galveston hurricane") subsequently devastated that city, killing a number estimated at from 8,000 to 12,000 in the deadliest [natural disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_disaster "Natural disaster") in U.S. history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-deadhurr-1)[\[108\]](#refnote-deadhurr) In 2017, [Hurricane Harvey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Harvey "Hurricane Harvey") caused a landfall in [Rockport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockport,_Texas "Rockport, Texas") as a Category4 Hurricane, causing significant damage, with its unprecedented amounts of rain over the [Greater Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Houston "Greater Houston") area resulting in widespread and catastrophic flooding, affecting hundreds of thousands of homes. Harvey ultimately became the costliest hurricane worldwide, causing an estimated $198.6billion in damage, surpassing the cost of [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina "Hurricane Katrina").[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-173)

Other devastating Texas hurricanes include the [1915 Galveston hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1915_Galveston_hurricane "1915 Galveston hurricane"), [Hurricane Audrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Audrey "Hurricane Audrey") in 1957, [Hurricane Carla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Carla "Hurricane Carla") in 1961, [Hurricane Beulah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Beulah "Hurricane Beulah") in 1967, [Hurricane Alicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Alicia "Hurricane Alicia") in 1983, [Hurricane Rita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Rita "Hurricane Rita") in 2005, and [Hurricane Ike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Ike "Hurricane Ike") in 2008. [Tropical storms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_cyclone "Tropical cyclone") have also caused their share of damage: [Allison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Storm_Allison_(1989) "Tropical Storm Allison (1989)") in 1989 and again [during 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Storm_Allison "Tropical Storm Allison"), [Claudette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Storm_Claudette_(1979) "Tropical Storm Claudette (1979)") in 1979, and [Tropical Storm Imelda in 2019.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Storm_Imelda "Tropical Storm Imelda")[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-174)[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-175)[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-176)

There is no substantial physical barrier between Texas and the [polar region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_regions_of_Earth "Polar regions of Earth"), and although rare, it is possible for arctic or polar [air masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_mass "Air mass") to penetrate Texas,[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-177)[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-178) as occurred during the [February 13–17, 2021 North American winter storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_13%E2%80%9317,_2021_North_American_winter_storm "February 13–17, 2021 North American winter storm").[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-179)[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-180) Usually, prevailing winds in North America push polar air masses to the southeast before they reach Texas. Because such incidents are rare, and, generally unexpected, they may result in crises such as the [2021 Texas power crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Texas_power_crisis "2021 Texas power crisis").

### Greenhouse gases

Main article: [Climate change in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Texas "Climate change in Texas")

As of 2017[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Texas&action=edit), Texas emitted the most [greenhouse gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas "Greenhouse gas") in the U.S.[&#8202;](#ref-back-eia17-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-eia17)As of 2017[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Texas&action=edit) the state emits about 1,600billion pounds (707million metric tons) of carbon dioxide annually.[&#8202;](#ref-back-eia17-1)[\[176\]](#refnote-eia17) As an independent state, Texas would rank as the world's seventh-largest producer of greenhouse gases.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GH2-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-GH2) Causes of the state's vast greenhouse gas emissions include the state's large number of [coal power plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_power_plant "Fossil fuel power plant") and the state's refining and manufacturing industries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GH2-1)[\[177\]](#refnote-GH2) In 2010, there were 2,553 "emission events" which poured 44.6million pounds (20,200 metric tons) of contaminants into the Texas sky.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-183)

### Administrative divisions

See also: [List of counties in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Texas "List of counties in Texas"), [List of Texas metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_metropolitan_areas "List of Texas metropolitan areas"), and [List of municipalities in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Texas "List of municipalities in Texas")

**[Largest city in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Texas "List of municipalities in Texas") by year[&#8202;](#ref-back-Census_Largest_Cities-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-Census_Largest_Cities)**

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Texas_municipalities.svg/500px-Texas_municipalities.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Texas_municipalities.svg) [Texas Counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Texas "List of counties in Texas") and [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Texas "List of municipalities in Texas")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Dirt_road_texas.jpg/500px-Dirt_road_texas.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dirt_road_texas.jpg) [Colonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_(United_States) "Colonia (United States)") in the [Rio Grande Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande_Valley_(Texas) "Rio Grande Valley (Texas)") near the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border "Mexico–United States border")

The state has three cities with populations exceeding one million: Houston, San Antonio, and Dallas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-188) These three rank among the 10 most populous cities of the United States. As of 2020, six Texas cities had populations greater than 600,000. Austin, Fort Worth, and El Paso are among the 20 [largest U.S. cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population "List of United States cities by population"). Texas has four [metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_metropolitan_areas "List of Texas metropolitan areas") with populations greater than a million: [Dallas–Fort Worth–Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth_Metroplex "Dallas–Fort Worth Metroplex"), [Houston–Sugar Land–The Woodlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Houston "Greater Houston"), [San Antonio–New Braunfels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_San_Antonio "Greater San Antonio"), and [Austin–Round Rock–San Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Austin "Greater Austin"). The Dallas–Fort Worth and Houston metropolitan areas number about 7.5 million and 7 million residents as of 2019, respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-189)

Three [interstate highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highway_System "Interstate Highway System")— [I-35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_35_(Texas) "Interstate 35 (Texas)") to the west (Dallas–Fort Worth to San Antonio, with Austin in between), [I-45](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_45_(Texas) "Interstate 45 (Texas)") to the east (Dallas to Houston), and [I-10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_10_(Texas) "Interstate 10 (Texas)") to the south (San Antonio to Houston) define the [Texas Urban Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Urban_Triangle "Texas Urban Triangle") region. The region of 60,000 square miles (160,000km2) contains most of the state's largest cities and metropolitan areas as well as 17 million people, nearly 75 percent of Texas's total population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-190) Houston and Dallas have been recognized as [world cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city "Global city").[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-191) These cities are spread out amongst the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-192)

In contrast to the cities, unincorporated rural settlements known as [colonias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_(border_settlement) "Colonia (border settlement)") often lack basic infrastructure and are marked by poverty.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Colonias-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-Colonias) The office of the Texas Attorney General stated, in 2011, that Texas had about 2,294 colonias, and estimates about 500,000 lived in the colonias. [Hidalgo County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hidalgo_County,_Texas "Hidalgo County, Texas"), as of 2011, has the largest number of colonias.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-194) Texas has the largest number of people living in [colonias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_(United_States) "Colonia (United States)") of all states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Colonias-1)[\[188\]](#refnote-Colonias)

Texas has [254 counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Texas "List of counties in Texas"), more than any other state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-195) Each county runs on [Commissioners' Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioners'_Court "Commissioners' Court") system consisting of four elected commissioners (one from each of four precincts in the county, roughly divided according to population) and a county judge elected at large from the entire county. County government runs similar to a "weak" [mayor-council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor-council "Mayor-council") system; the county judge has no veto authority, but votes along with the other commissioners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-auto)

Although Texas permits cities and counties to enter "interlocal agreements" to share services, the state does not allow [consolidated city-county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_city-county "Consolidated city-county") governments, nor does it have [metropolitan governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_government "Metropolitan government"). Counties are not granted [home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_rule "Home rule") status; their powers are strictly defined by state law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-198)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-1)[\[192\]](#refnote-auto) The state does not have townships—areas within a county are either incorporated or unincorporated. Incorporated areas are part of a municipality. The county provides limited services to unincorporated areas and to some smaller incorporated areas. Municipalities are classified either "general law" cities or "home rule".[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-199) A municipality may elect home rule status once it exceeds 5,000 population with voter approval.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-200)

Texas also permits the creation of "special districts", which provide limited services. The most common is the [school district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district "School district"), but can also include hospital districts, community college districts, and utility districts. Municipal, school district, and special district elections are [nonpartisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-partisan_democracy "Non-partisan democracy"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-201) though the party affiliation of a candidate may be well-known. County and state elections are partisan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-202)

Historical population[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Texas_population_density_2020.png/500px-Texas_population_density_2020.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Texas_population_density_2020.png) Texas population density map

The resident population of Texas was 29,145,505 in the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census "2020 United States census"), a 15.9% increase since the [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census "2010 United States census").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Historical_Population_Change_Data-1)[\[199\]](#refnote-Historical_Population_Change_Data) At the 2020 census, the apportioned population of Texas stood at 29,183,290.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-205) The [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau "United States Census Bureau") estimated the population was 31,290,831 as of July 1, 2024, an increase of 7.4% since the 2020 census.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CensusQuickFacts-2)[\[3\]](#refnote-CensusQuickFacts) Texas is the second-most populous state in the United States after California and the only other U.S. state to surpass a total estimated population of 30 million people as of July 2, 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-206)[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-207)

In 2015, Texas had 4.7 million foreign-born residents, about 17% of the population and 21.6% of the state workforce.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AICTexas-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-AICTexas) The major countries of origin for Texan immigrants were Mexico (55.1% of immigrants), India (5%), El Salvador (4.3%), Vietnam (3.7%), and China (2.3%).[&#8202;](#ref-back-AICTexas-1)[\[203\]](#refnote-AICTexas) Of immigrant residents, 35.8 percent were [naturalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization "Naturalization") U.S. citizens.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AICTexas-2)[\[203\]](#refnote-AICTexas) As of 2018, the population increased to 4.9 million foreign-born residents or 17.2% of the state population, up from 2,899,642 in 2000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-209)

In 2014, there were an estimated 1.7 million undocumented immigrants in Texas, making up 35% of the total Texas immigrant population and 6.1% of the total state population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AICTexas-3)[\[203\]](#refnote-AICTexas) In addition to the state's foreign-born population, 4.1 million Texans (15% of the state's population) were born in the United States yet had at least one immigrant parent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AICTexas-4)[\[203\]](#refnote-AICTexas) According to the [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey "American Community Survey")'s 2019 estimates, 1,739,000 residents were undocumented immigrants, a decrease of 103,000 since 2014 but an increase of 142,000 since 2016. Of the undocumented immigrant population, 951,000 had resided in Texas no more than 14 years; an estimated 788,000 had lived in Texas for 15 or more years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-210)

Texas's [Rio Grande Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande_Valley_(Texas) "Rio Grande Valley (Texas)") has seen significant migration from across the [U.S.–Mexico border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93Mexico_border "U.S.–Mexico border"). During the [2014 crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_American_immigration_crisis "2014 American immigration crisis"), many [Central Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_America "Central America"), including unaccompanied minors traveling alone from [Guatemala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guatemala "Guatemala"), [Honduras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honduras "Honduras"), and [El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Salvador "El Salvador"), reached the state, overwhelming Border Patrol resources for a time. Many sought [asylum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_asylum "Right of asylum") in the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-211)[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-212)

Texas's population density as of 2020 is 110 peopleper square mile (42 people/km2) which is slightly higher than the average [population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population_density "List of countries by population density") of the U.S. as a whole, at 93 peopleper square mile (36 people/km2). In contrast, while Texas and France are similarly sized geographically, the European country has a population density of 256 peopleper square mile (99 people/km2). Using [population-weighted density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population-weighted_density "Population-weighted density")[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[note 1\]](#refnote-213), Texas has a density of 3,604 peopleper square mile (1,392 people/km2), lower than the U.S. average, at 5,217 peopleper square mile (2,014 people/km2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-214)[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-215). Two-thirds of all Texans live in major metropolitan areas such as Houston.

According to [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development "United States Department of Housing and Urban Development")'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress "Annual Homeless Assessment Report to Congress"), there were an estimated 24,432 [homeless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness "Homelessness") people in Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-216)[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-217)

**Texas – racial and ethnic composition**

_Note: The U.S. Census treats Hispanic/Latino as an ethnic category. This table excludes Latinos from the racial categories and assigns them to a separate category. Hispanics/Latinos may be of any race._[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Ethnic_origins_in_Texas_%282020%29.jpg/500px-Ethnic_origins_in_Texas_%282020%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ethnic_origins_in_Texas_(2020).jpg) Largest alone or in any combination ethnic origin by county in Texas, per the 2020 census[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-223)

In 2019, [non-Hispanic Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_or_Latino_whites "Non-Hispanic or Latino whites") represented 41.2% of Texas's population, reflecting a national demographic shift.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-224)[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-226) [Black people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans_in_Texas "African Americans in Texas") made up 12.9%, [American Indians and Alaska Natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_Texas "Native Americans in Texas") 1.0%, [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans "Asian Americans") 5.2%, [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians "Native Hawaiians") and other [Pacific Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander_Americans "Pacific Islander Americans") 0.1%, some other race 0.2%, and [two or more races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_Americans "Multiracial Americans") 1.8%. [Hispanics or Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanics_and_Latinos_in_Texas "Hispanics and Latinos in Texas") of any race made up 39.7% of the estimated population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-227)

At the 2020 census, the racial and ethnic composition of the state was 42.5% [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans "White Americans") (39.8% non-Hispanic White), 11.8% Black, 5.4% Asian, 0.3% American Indian and Alaska Native, 0.1% Native Hawaiian and other Pacific Islander, 13.6% some other race, 17.6% two or more races, and 40.2% Hispanic and Latino American of any race. Minority Americans make up 60.2% of the Texas population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-228)[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-229)

In 2010, 49% of all births were Hispanics; 35% were non-Hispanic White; 11.5% were non-Hispanic Black, and 4.3% were Asians/Pacific Islanders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-230) Based on U.S. Census Bureau data released in February 2011, for the first time in recent history, Texas's White population is below 50% (45%) and Hispanics grew to 38%. Between 2000 and 2010, the total population grew by 20.6%, but Hispanics and Latino Americans grew by 65%, whereas non-Hispanic Whites grew by only 4.2%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-231) Texas has the fifth highest rate of teenage births in the nation and a plurality of these are to Hispanics or Latinos.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-232)[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-233) As of 2022, Hispanics and Latinos of any race replaced the non-Hispanic White population as the largest share of the state's population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-234)

Texas has the second-largest share of [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans "Mexican Americans") in the U.S., making up 32.2% of the total population and 80% of the state's Hispanic population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-censusreporter.org-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-censusreporter.org) Other than Mexican, the largest self-reported ancestries in the state as of 2022 were [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans "German Americans") (8.1%), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans "English Americans") (7.9%), [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans "Irish Americans") (5.8%), those identifying as [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_ancestry "American ancestry") (4.6%), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Americans "Italian Americans") (1.9%), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Americans "Indian Americans") (1.9%), [Salvadoran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_Americans "Salvadoran Americans") (1.4%), [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Americans "Scottish Americans") (1.3%), [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_Americans "Vietnamese Americans") (1.1%), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Americans "Chinese Americans") (1%), [Puerto Rican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Ricans "Puerto Ricans") (0.9%), [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Americans "Polish Americans") (0.9%), [Honduran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honduran_Americans "Honduran Americans") (0.8%), [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Americans "Filipino Americans") (0.8%), and [Scotch-Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotch-Irish_Americans "Scotch-Irish Americans") (0.7%).[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-236)[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-237)[&#8202;](#ref-back-censusreporter.org-1)[\[228\]](#refnote-censusreporter.org)

Most common non-English languages

The most common [accent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accent_(sociolinguistics) "Accent (sociolinguistics)") or [dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialect "Dialect") spoken by natives throughout Texas is sometimes referred to as [Texan English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texan_English "Texan English"), itself a sub-variety of a broader category of [American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English "American English") known as [Southern American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_American_English "Southern American English").[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-239)[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-240) [Creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language "Creole language") is spoken in some parts of East Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-241) In some areas of the state—particularly in the large cities— [Western American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_American_English "Western American English") and [General American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_American_English "General American English"), is increasingly common. [Chicano English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicano_English "Chicano English")—due to a growing Hispanic population—is widespread in South Texas, while [African-American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_English "African-American English") is especially notable in historically minority areas of urban Texas.

At the 2020 American Community Survey's estimates, 64.9% of the population of Texas spoke only English, while 35.1% spoke a language other than English.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-242) Roughly 30% of the total population spoke Spanish. By 2021, approximately 50,546 Texans spoke French or a [French-based creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French-based_creole_language "French-based creole language"). German and other West Germanic languages were spoken by 49,565 residents; Russian, Polish, and other Slavic languages by 37,444; Korean by 31,673; Chinese 86,370; Vietnamese 92,410; Tagalog 40,124; and Arabic by 47,170 Texans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-243)

At the census of 2010, 65.8% (14,740,304) of Texas residents age5 and older spoke only [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language") at home, while 29.2% (6,543,702) spoke [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language "Spanish language"), 0.8 percent (168,886) [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_language "Vietnamese language"), and [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language") (which includes [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese "Cantonese") and [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese "Standard Chinese")) was spoken by 0.6% (122,921) of the population over five.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MLA_Data-1)[\[231\]](#refnote-MLA_Data) Other languages spoken include [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language "German language") (including [Texas German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_German "Texas German")) by 0.3% (73,137), [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language "Tagalog language") with 0.3% (64,272) speakers, and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language") (including [Cajun French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajun_French "Cajun French")) was spoken by 0.3% (55,773) of Texans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MLA_Data-2)[\[231\]](#refnote-MLA_Data) Reportedly, [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee_language "Cherokee language") is the most widely spoken [Native American language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_language "Native American language") in Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-244) In total, 34.2% (7,660,406) of Texas's population aged five and older spoke a language at home other than English as of 2006.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MLA_Data-3)[\[231\]](#refnote-MLA_Data)

Religious affiliation (2020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-245)

With the coming of Spanish Catholic and American Protestant missionary societies,[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-246) [American Indian religions and spiritual traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_religions "Native American religions") dwindled. Since then, colonial and present-day Texas has become a predominantly Christian state, with 75.5% of the population identifying as such according to the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute "Public Religion Research Institute") in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-American_Values_Atlas-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-American_Values_Atlas)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/St._Mary%27s_Cathedral_Basilica_-_Galveston_02.jpg/500px-St._Mary%27s_Cathedral_Basilica_-_Galveston_02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:St._Mary's_Cathedral_Basilica_-_Galveston_02.jpg) [St. Mary's Cathedral Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary_Cathedral_Basilica_(Galveston,_Texas) "St. Mary Cathedral Basilica (Galveston, Texas)") of the [Archdiocese of Galveston-Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Galveston%E2%80%93Houston "Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Galveston–Houston")

Among its majority Christian populace, the largest Christian denomination as of 2014 has been the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church"), per the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center") at 23% of the population, although [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism "Protestantism") collectively constituted 50% of the Christian population in 2014;[&#8202;](#ref-back-Religion_in_America-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-Religion_in_America) in the 2020 study by the Public Religion Research Institute, the Catholic Church's membership increased to encompassing 28% of the population identifying with a religious or spiritual belief.[&#8202;](#ref-back-American_Values_Atlas-1)[\[240\]](#refnote-American_Values_Atlas) At the 2020 [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives "Association of Religion Data Archives") study, there were 5,905,142 Catholics in the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-U.S._Religion_Census-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-U.S._Religion_Census) The largest Catholic jurisdictions in Texas are the [Archdiocese of Galveston–Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Galveston%E2%80%93Houston "Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Galveston–Houston")—the first and oldest [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church "Latin Church") diocese in Texas[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-250)—the dioceses of [Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Dallas "Roman Catholic Diocese of Dallas") and [Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Fort_Worth "Roman Catholic Diocese of Fort Worth"), and the [Archdiocese of San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_San_Antonio "Roman Catholic Archdiocese of San Antonio").

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Dallas_-_First_Baptist_Church_02.jpg/500px-Dallas_-_First_Baptist_Church_02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dallas_-_First_Baptist_Church_02.jpg) [First Baptist Church of Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Baptist_Church_(Dallas) "First Baptist Church (Dallas)")

Being part of the strongly, socially conservative [Bible Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Belt "Bible Belt"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-251) Protestants as a whole declined to 47% of the population in the 2020 study by the Public Religion Research Institute. Predominantly-white [Evangelical Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism "Evangelicalism") declined to 14% of the Protestant Christian population. [Mainline Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestant "Mainline Protestant") in contrast made up 15% of Protestant Texas. Hispanic or Latino American-dominated Protestant churches and [historically Black or African American Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_church "Black church") grew to a collective 13% of the Protestant population.

Evangelical Protestants were 31% of the population in 2014, and [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists "Baptists") were the largest Evangelical tradition (14%);[&#8202;](#ref-back-Religion_in_America-1)[\[241\]](#refnote-Religion_in_America) according to the 2014 study, they made up the second-largest Mainline Protestant group behind [Methodists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodism "Methodism") (4%). [Nondenominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity "Nondenominational Christianity") and interdenominational Protestant Christians were the second largest Evangelical group (7%) followed by [Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism "Pentecostalism") (4%). The largest Evangelical Baptists in the state were the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention "Southern Baptist Convention") (9%) and independent Baptists (3%). The [Assemblies of God USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblies_of_God_USA "Assemblies of God USA") was the largest Evangelical Pentecostal denomination in 2014. Among [Mainline Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestants "Mainline Protestants"), the [United Methodist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Methodist_Church "United Methodist Church") was the largest denomination (4%) and the [American Baptist Churches USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Baptist_Churches_USA "American Baptist Churches USA") comprised the second-largest Mainline Protestant group (2%).

According to the Pew Research Center in 2014, the state's largest historically African American Christian denominations were the [National Baptist Convention (USA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baptist_Convention,_USA,_Inc. "National Baptist Convention, USA, Inc.") and the [Church of God in Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_God_in_Christ "Church of God in Christ"). Black Methodists and other Christians made up less than 1 percent each of the Christian demographic. Other Christians made up 1 percent of the total Christian population, and the [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church "Eastern Orthodox Church") and [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches "Oriental Orthodox Churches") formed less than 1 percent of the statewide Christian populace. The [Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints_in_Texas "The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints in Texas") is the largest [nontrinitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nontrinitarianism "Nontrinitarianism") Christian group in Texas alongside the [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses "Jehovah's Witnesses").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Religion_in_America-2)[\[241\]](#refnote-Religion_in_America)

Among its Protestant population, the Association of Religion Data Archives in 2020 determined Southern Baptists numbered 3,319,962; non-denominational Protestants 2,405,786 (including [Christian Churches and Churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_churches_and_churches_of_Christ "Christian churches and churches of Christ"), and the [Churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churches_of_Christ "Churches of Christ") altogether numbering 2,758,353); and United Methodists 938,399 as the most numerous Protestant groups in the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-U.S._Religion_Census-1)[\[242\]](#refnote-U.S._Religion_Census) Baptists altogether (Southern Baptists, [American Baptist Associates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Baptist_Association "American Baptist Association"), American Baptists, [Full Gospel Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_Gospel_Baptist_Church_Fellowship "Full Gospel Baptist Church Fellowship"), [General Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Baptists "General Baptists"), [Free Will Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Will_Baptist "Free Will Baptist"), National Baptists, [National Baptists of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baptist_Convention_of_America_International,_Inc. "National Baptist Convention of America International, Inc."), [National Missionary Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Missionary_Baptist_Convention_of_America "National Missionary Baptist Convention of America"), [National Primitive Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Primitive_Baptist_Convention_of_the_U.S.A. "National Primitive Baptist Convention of the U.S.A."), and [Progressive National Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_National_Baptist_Convention "Progressive National Baptist Convention")) numbered 3,837,306; Methodists within United Methodism, the [AME](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church "African Methodist Episcopal Church"), [AME Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Zion_Church "African Methodist Episcopal Zion Church"), [CME](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Methodist_Episcopal_Church "Christian Methodist Episcopal Church"), and the [Free Methodist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Methodist_Church "Free Methodist Church") numbered 1,026,453 Texans.

The same study tabulated 425,038 Pentecostals spread among the Assemblies of God, [Church of God (Cleveland)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_God_(Cleveland,_Tennessee) "Church of God (Cleveland, Tennessee)"), and Church of God in Christ. Nontrinitarian or [Oneness Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oneness_Pentecostalism "Oneness Pentecostalism") numbered 7,042 between [Bible Way Church of Our Lord Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Way_Church_of_Our_Lord_Jesus_Christ "Bible Way Church of Our Lord Jesus Christ"), [COOLJC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Our_Lord_Jesus_Christ_of_the_Apostolic_Faith "Church of Our Lord Jesus Christ of the Apostolic Faith"), and the [Pentecostal Assemblies of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal_Assemblies_of_the_World "Pentecostal Assemblies of the World"). Other Christians, including the Eastern and Oriental Orthodox, numbered 55,329 altogether, and Episcopalians numbered 134,318, although the [Anglican Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Catholic_Church "Anglican Catholic Church"), [Anglican Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church_in_America "Anglican Church in America"), [Anglican Church in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church_in_North_America "Anglican Church in North America"), [Anglican Province of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Province_of_America "Anglican Province of America"), and [Holy Catholic Church Anglican Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Catholic_Church_(Anglican_Rite) "Holy Catholic Church (Anglican Rite)") had a collective presence in 114 churches.[&#8202;](#ref-back-U.S._Religion_Census-2)[\[242\]](#refnote-U.S._Religion_Census)

Non-Christian faiths accounted for 4% of the religious population in 2014, and 5% in 2020 per the Pew Research Center and Public Religion Research Institute.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Religion_in_America-3)[\[241\]](#refnote-Religion_in_America)[&#8202;](#ref-back-American_Values_Atlas-2)[\[240\]](#refnote-American_Values_Atlas) Adherents of many other religions reside predominantly in the urban centers of Texas. Judaism, Islam, and Buddhism were tied as the second largest religion as of 2014 and 2020. In 2014, 18% of the state's population were religiously unaffiliated. Of the unaffiliated in 2014, an estimated 2% were [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism "Atheism") and 3% [agnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism "Agnosticism"); in 2020, the Public Religion Research Institute noted the largest non-Christian groups were the [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_but_not_religious "Spiritual but not religious") (20%), [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism "Judaism") (1%), [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") (1%), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") (1%) and [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism"), and other religions at less than 1 percent each.

In 1990, the Islamic population was about 140,000 with more recent figures putting the current number of Muslims between 350,000 and 400,000 as of 2012.[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-252) The Association of Religion Data Archives estimated there were 313,209 Muslims as of 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-U.S._Religion_Census-3)[\[242\]](#refnote-U.S._Religion_Census) Texas is the fifth-largest [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim "Muslim")-populated state as of 2014.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-253) The Jewish population was around 128,000 in 2008.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-254) In 2020, the Jewish population grew to over 176,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-255) According to ARDA's 2020 study, there were 43 [Chabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chabad "Chabad") synagogues; 17,513 [Conservative Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Jews "Conservative Jews"); 8,110 [Orthodox Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Jews "Orthodox Jews"); and 31,378 [Reform Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism "Reform Judaism"). Around 146,000 adherents of religions such as [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") and [Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism "Sikhism") lived in Texas as of 2004.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-256) By 2020, there were 112,153 Hindus and 20 Sikh gurdwaras; 60,882 Texans adhered to [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism").

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Texas "Economy of Texas")

See also: [Texas locations by per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_locations_by_per_capita_income "Texas locations by per capita income") and [Texas Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Stock_Exchange "Texas Stock Exchange")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9a/Geo_Map_of_Income_by_Location_in_Texas_%282014%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Geo_Map_of_Income_by_Location_in_Texas_(2014).png) A geomap depicting income by county as of 2014[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Texas_counties_by_GDP_2021.png/500px-Texas_counties_by_GDP_2021.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Texas_counties_by_GDP_2021.png) Texas counties by GDP (2021)

As of 2025, Texas had a [gross state product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_state_product "Gross state product") (GSP) of $2.904 trillion, the [second highest of any U.S. state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_GDP_(nominal) "List of U.S. states by GDP (nominal)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-2025Economy-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-2025Economy) In 2025, Texas's per capita personal income was $72,364.[&#8202;](#ref-back-2025Economy-1)[\[250\]](#refnote-2025Economy) Its GSP is greater than the GDP of [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Brazil "Economy of Brazil"), the world's [8th-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal) "List of countries by GDP (nominal)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-258) The [poverty rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_the_United_States "Poverty in the United States") is 14.2%, making Texas the state with 14th highest poverty rate (compared to 13.15% nationally). Texas's economy is the [second-largest of any country subdivision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_country_subdivisions_by_GDP_over_200_billion_USD "List of country subdivisions by GDP over 200 billion USD") globally, behind [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_California "Economy of California"). According to _Business Observer_, Texas ranked third among U.S. states for business formations in 2025, with 457,373 new businesses formed within the state that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-259)

Texas's large population, natural resources, cities, and centers of higher education have contributed to a large and diverse economy. Since oil was discovered, the state's economy has reflected the state of the [petroleum industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Texas#Petroleum "Energy in Texas"). In recent times, urban centers of the state have increased in size, containing two-thirds of the population in 2005. The state's economic growth has led to [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl "Urban sprawl") and its associated symptoms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-260)

As of May 2025, the unemployment rate was 4.1%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-:1)

In 2010, _Site Selection Magazine_ ranked Texas as the most business-friendly state, in part because of the state's three-billion-dollar [Texas Enterprise Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Enterprise_Fund "Texas Enterprise Fund").[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-262) As of 2024, it has the [second-highest number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_number_of_Fortune_500_companies "List of U.S. states by number of Fortune 500 companies") (52) of [_Fortune_ 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500 "Fortune 500") companies headquartered in the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-:0) In 2010, there were 346,000 millionaires in Texas, the second-largest population of millionaires in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[g\]](#refnote-264)[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-265) In 2018, the number of [millionaire households](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_the_number_of_millionaire_households "List of U.S. states by the number of millionaire households") increased to 566,578.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-266)

Small businesses, or businesses that employ fewer than 500 people, make up 99.8% of businesses in Texas and employ approximately 5.1 million people, as of December 2025. The number of small businesses increased by 24% from 2017 and 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-267)

### Taxation

Texas has a reputation for low taxes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TPoliticalCulture1-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-TPoliticalCulture1) According to the [Tax Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Foundation "Tax Foundation"), Texans' state and local tax burdens are seventh-lowest nationally; state and local taxes cost $3,580 per capita, or 8.4 percent of resident incomes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TaxFound-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-TaxFound) Texas is one of seven states that lack a [state income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_income_tax "State income tax").[&#8202;](#ref-back-TaxFound-1)[\[261\]](#refnote-TaxFound)[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-270)

Instead, the state collects revenue from [property taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax "Property tax") (though these are collected at the county, city, and school district level; Texas has a state constitutional prohibition against a state property tax) and [sales taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax "Sales tax"). The state sales tax rate is 6.25 percent,[&#8202;](#ref-back-TaxFound-2)[\[261\]](#refnote-TaxFound)[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-271) but local taxing jurisdictions (cities, counties, special purpose districts, and transit authorities) may also impose sales and use tax up to 2percent for a total maximum combined rate of 8.25 percent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-272) Gasoline tax, as of 2025, is 38.40 cents per gallon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[254\]](#refnote-:1)

Texas receives more federal spending than it pays in federal taxes, receiving $1.21 for each dollar paid to the federal government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-273) To attract business, Texas has incentive programs worth $19 billion per year (2012); more than any other U.S. state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-274)[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-275)

### Agriculture and mining

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/West_Texas_Cotton.jpg/500px-West_Texas_Cotton.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:West_Texas_Cotton.jpg) Cotton modules after harvest in West Texas[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/67/Longhorn_cattle_at_the_Enchanted_Springs_Ranch%2C_a_working_ranch_in_Boerne%2C_Texas%2C_whose_Old_West_Town_is_often_used_in_movies_and_videos_LCCN2015630429.tif/lossy-page1-500px-Longhorn_cattle_at_the_Enchanted_Springs_Ranch%2C_a_working_ranch_in_Boerne%2C_Texas%2C_whose_Old_West_Town_is_often_used_in_movies_and_videos_LCCN2015630429.tif.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Longhorn_cattle_at_the_Enchanted_Springs_Ranch,_a_working_ranch_in_Boerne,_Texas,_whose_Old_West_Town_is_often_used_in_movies_and_videos_LCCN2015630429.tif) [Texas longhorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_longhorn "Texas longhorn") cattle in [Boerne, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boerne,_Texas "Boerne, Texas")

Texas has the most farms and the highest acreage in the United States. The state is ranked No.1 for revenue generated from total livestock and livestock products. It is ranked No.2 for total agricultural revenue, behind California.[&#8202;](#ref-back-netstateecon-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-netstateecon) As of 2021, Texas had 126 million acres of farmland, which is the most out of all U.S. states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-:2) At $7.4 billion or 56.7 percent of Texas's annual agricultural cash receipts, beef cattle production represents the largest single segment of Texas agriculture. This is followed by cotton at $1.9 billion (14.6 percent), greenhouse/nursery at $1.5 billion (11.4 percent), [broiler chickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broiler_chicken "Broiler chicken") at $1.3 billion (10 percent), and dairy products at $947 million (7.3 percent).[&#8202;](#ref-back-EMMA2013-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-EMMA2013)

Texas leads the nation in the production of cattle, horses, sheep, goats, wool, mohair and hay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-EMMA2013-1)[\[270\]](#refnote-EMMA2013) The state also leads the nation in production of cotton which is the number one crop grown in the state in terms of value.[&#8202;](#ref-back-netstateecon-1)[\[268\]](#refnote-netstateecon)[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-279)[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-280) The state grows significant amounts of cereal crops and produce.[&#8202;](#ref-back-netstateecon-2)[\[268\]](#refnote-netstateecon) Texas's fishing industry, along the [Texas Gulf Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Gulf_Coast "Texas Gulf Coast"), is vital to Texas's economy. In 2015, there were 80.4 million pounds of seafood landed, valued at more than $173.4 million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-281) With mineral resources, Texas leads in creating cement, crushed stone, lime, salt, sand and gravel.[&#8202;](#ref-back-netstateecon-3)[\[268\]](#refnote-netstateecon) Texas throughout the 21st century has been [heavily impacted by drought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drought_in_the_United_States "Drought in the United States"), costing the state billions of dollars in livestock and crops.[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-282) Farmland has been reduced by non-agricultural development, which includes the construction of data centers. 1.8 million acres of farmland was converted between 2017 and 2022, according to _[The Texas Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Texas_Tribune "The Texas Tribune")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[269\]](#refnote-:2)

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Texas "Energy in Texas")

See also: [Deregulation of the Texas electricity market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation_of_the_Texas_electricity_market "Deregulation of the Texas electricity market")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Oil_well.jpg/500px-Oil_well.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Oil_well.jpg) An oil well[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/GreenMountainWindFarm_Fluvanna_2004.jpg/500px-GreenMountainWindFarm_Fluvanna_2004.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GreenMountainWindFarm_Fluvanna_2004.jpg) [Brazos Wind Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazos_Wind_Farm "Brazos Wind Farm")

Ever since the discovery of oil at [Spindletop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spindletop "Spindletop"), energy has been a dominant force politically and economically within the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-283) If Texas were its own country it would be the sixth-largest oil producer in the world according to a 2014 study.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-284)

The [Railroad Commission of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_Commission_of_Texas "Railroad Commission of Texas") regulates the state's [oil and gas industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_industry "Oil industry"), gas utilities, pipeline safety, safety in the [liquefied petroleum gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquefied_petroleum_gas "Liquefied petroleum gas") industry, and surface coal and [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") mining. Until the 1970s, the commission controlled the price of petroleum because of its ability to regulate Texas's oil reserves. The founders of the [Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Petroleum_Exporting_Countries "Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries") (OPEC) used the Texas agency as one of their models for petroleum price control.[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-285)

As of January 1, 2021, Texas has [proved recoverable petroleum reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_and_gas_reserves_and_resource_quantification "Oil and gas reserves and resource quantification") of about 15.6 billion barrels (2.48×109m3) of [crude oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum "Petroleum") (44% of the known U.S. reserves) and 9.5 billion barrels (1.51×109m3) of [natural gas liquids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural-gas_condensate "Natural-gas condensate").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol)[&#8202;](#ref-back-eia.gov-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-eia.gov) The state's [refineries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_refinery "Oil refinery") can process 5.95 million barrels (946,000m3) of oil a day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-1)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol)[&#8202;](#ref-back-eia.gov-1)[\[279\]](#refnote-eia.gov) The [Port Arthur Refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Arthur_Refinery "Port Arthur Refinery") in Southeast Texas is the largest refinery in the U.S.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-2)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol) Texas is also a leader in [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas "Natural gas") production at 28.8billion cubic feet (820,000,000m3) per day, some 32% of the nation's production.[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-288) Texas has 102.4trillion cubic feet (2.90×1012m3) of gas reserves which is 23% of the nation's gas reserves.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-3)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol)[&#8202;](#ref-back-eia.gov-2)[\[279\]](#refnote-eia.gov) Many [petroleum companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_petroleum_companies "List of petroleum companies") are based in Texas such as: [ConocoPhillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ConocoPhillips "ConocoPhillips"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-289) [EOG Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EOG_Resources "EOG Resources"), [ExxonMobil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ExxonMobil "ExxonMobil"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-290) [Halliburton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halliburton "Halliburton"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-291) Hilcorp, [Marathon Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathon_Oil "Marathon Oil"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-292) [Occidental Petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occidental_Petroleum "Occidental Petroleum"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-293) [Valero Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valero_Energy "Valero Energy"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-294) and [Western Refining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Refining "Western Refining").[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-295)

As of 2023, Texans consume, on average, the sixth most energy (of all types) in the nation per capita, following Alaska, Louisiana, North Dakota, Wyoming and West Virginia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-4)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol)

Unlike the rest of the nation, most of Texas is on its own [alternating current](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternating_current "Alternating current") [power grid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_grid "Power grid"), the [Texas Interconnection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Interconnection "Texas Interconnection"). Texas has a [deregulated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation_of_the_Texas_electricity_market "Deregulation of the Texas electricity market") electric service. Texas leads the nation in total net electricity production, generating 437,236 MWh in 2014, 89% more MWh than Florida, which ranked second.[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-296)[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-297)

The state is a leader in [renewable energy commercialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_commercialization "Renewable energy commercialization"); it produces the most [wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_Texas "Wind power in Texas") in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-5)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol)[&#8202;](#ref-back-298-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-298) In 2014, 10.6% of the electricity consumed in Texas came from [wind turbines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_turbine "Wind turbine").[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-299) The [Roscoe Wind Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roscoe_Wind_Farm "Roscoe Wind Farm") in [Roscoe, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roscoe,_Texas "Roscoe, Texas"), is one of the world's largest [wind farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_farm "Wind farm") with a 781.5 [megawatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megawatt "Megawatt") (MW) capacity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-300) The Energy Information Administration states the state's large agriculture and forestry industries could give Texas an enormous amount of [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass "Biomass") for use in biofuels. The state also has the highest [solar power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_power "Solar power") potential for development in the U.S.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Petrol-6)[\[278\]](#refnote-Petrol)

### Technology

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/NASA_Neutral_Buoyancy_Laboratory_Astronaut_Training.jpg/500px-NASA_Neutral_Buoyancy_Laboratory_Astronaut_Training.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NASA_Neutral_Buoyancy_Laboratory_Astronaut_Training.jpg) Astronaut training at the [Johnson Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Space_Center "Johnson Space Center") in [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston "Houston")

With large universities systems coupled with initiatives like the Texas Enterprise Fund and the [Texas Emerging Technology Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Emerging_Technology_Fund "Texas Emerging Technology Fund"), a wide array of different [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_tech "High tech") industries have developed in Texas. The Austin area is nicknamed the " [Silicon Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Hills "Silicon Hills")" and the north Dallas area the " [Silicon Prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Prairie "Silicon Prairie")". Many high-tech companies are located in or have their headquarters in Texas (and [Austin in particular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_based_in_Austin,_Texas "List of companies based in Austin, Texas")), including [Dell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dell "Dell"), Inc.,[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-301) [Borland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borland "Borland"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-302) [Forcepoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forcepoint "Forcepoint"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-303) [Indeed.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indeed.com "Indeed.com"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-304) [Texas Instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Instruments "Texas Instruments"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-305) [Perot Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perot_Systems "Perot Systems"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-306) [Rackspace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rackspace "Rackspace") and [AT&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT&T "AT&T").[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-307)[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-308)[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-309)

The [National Aeronautics and Space Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Aeronautics_and_Space_Administration "National Aeronautics and Space Administration")'s [Lyndon B. Johnson Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson_Space_Center "Lyndon B. Johnson Space Center") (NASA JSC) is located in Southeast Houston. Both [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX") and [Blue Origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Origin "Blue Origin") have their test facilities in Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-310)[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-311) [Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Worth,_Texas "Fort Worth, Texas") hosts both [Lockheed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin "Lockheed Martin")'s [Aeronautics division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_Aeronautics "Lockheed Martin Aeronautics") and [Bell Helicopter Textron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_Helicopter_Textron "Bell Helicopter Textron").[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-312)[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-313) Lockheed builds the [F-16 Fighting Falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-16_Fighting_Falcon "F-16 Fighting Falcon"), the largest Western fighter program, and its successor, the [F-35 Lightning II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-35_Lightning_II "F-35 Lightning II") in Fort Worth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-314)

### Commerce

Texas has strong commercial sector consisting of [retail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retail "Retail"), [wholesale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wholesale "Wholesale"), banking and insurance, and construction sectors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Leagueown-1)[\[11\]](#refnote-Leagueown) Texas further has a diverse economy, with multiple _Fortune_ 500 companies not based on Texas traditional industries such as [AT&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT&T_Inc "AT&T Inc"), [Kimberly-Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberly-Clark "Kimberly-Clark"), [J. C. Penney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._C._Penney "J. C. Penney"), [Whole Foods Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_Foods_Market "Whole Foods Market"), and [Tenet Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenet_Healthcare "Tenet Healthcare").[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-315) Nationally, the Dallas–Fort Worth area, home to the [second shopping mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Park_Village "Highland Park Village") in the United States, has the most shopping malls per capita of any American metropolitan statistical area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-316)

Mexico, the state's largest trading partner, imports a third of the state's exports because of the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement "North American Free Trade Agreement") (NAFTA). NAFTA has encouraged the formation of [maquiladoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maquiladoras "Maquiladoras") on the Texas–Mexico border.[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-317)

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Texas "Transportation in Texas")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/High_Five.jpg/500px-High_Five.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:High_Five.jpg) The [High Five Interchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Five_Interchange "High Five Interchange") in [Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas "Dallas")

Although the state's large size and rough terrain have historically complicated transportation, Texas has compensated by building the nation's largest highway and railway systems. The [regulatory authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatory_authority "Regulatory authority"), the [Texas Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Department_of_Transportation "Texas Department of Transportation") (TxDOT), maintains the state's highway system, and regulates aviation and [public transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transportation "Public transportation") systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-318)[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-319)

Texas is an important [transportation hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_hub "Transportation hub") in America - from the Dallas/Fort Worth area, trucks can reach 93 percent of the nation's population within 48 hours, and 37 percent within 24 hours.[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-320) Texas has 33 [foreign trade zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Economic_Zone "Special Economic Zone") (FTZ), the most in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FTZ1-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-FTZ1) In 2004, a combined total of $298billion of goods passed through Texas FTZs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FTZ1-1)[\[313\]](#refnote-FTZ1)

#### Highways

Main article: [Texas state highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_state_highways "Texas state highways")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Welcome_to_Texas_sign%2C_2008.jpg/500px-Welcome_to_Texas_sign%2C_2008.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Welcome_to_Texas_sign,_2008.jpg)"Welcome to Texas" sign, entering the state from Arkansas on [Interstate 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_30 "Interstate 30")

The first Texas freeway was the [Gulf Freeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Freeway "Gulf Freeway") opened in 1948 in Houston.[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-322) As of 2005, 79,535 miles (127,999km) of public highway crisscrossed Texas (up from 71,000 miles or 114,000km in 1984).[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-323) To fund recent growth in the state highways, Texas has [17 toll roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Toll_Roads_in_the_United_States#Texas "List of Toll Roads in the United States") with several additional [tollways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tollway "Tollway") proposed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-324) In [Central Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Texas "Central Texas"), the southern section of the [State Highway 130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_Highway_130 "Texas State Highway 130") toll road has a speed limit of 85 miles per hour (137km/h), the highest in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-325) All federal and state highways in Texas are paved.

#### Airports

See also: [List of airports in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Texas "List of airports in Texas")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/20110123_DFW_terminal_D.jpg/500px-20110123_DFW_terminal_D.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:20110123_DFW_terminal_D.jpg) Terminal D at [Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas/Fort_Worth_International_Airport "Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Bush_terminal_E.jpg/500px-Bush_terminal_E.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bush_terminal_E.jpg) Terminal E at [George Bush Intercontinental Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bush_Intercontinental_Airport "George Bush Intercontinental Airport") in Houston

Texas has 730 airports, second-most of any state in the nation. Largest in Texas by size and passengers served, [Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas/Fort_Worth_International_Airport "Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport") (DFW) is the second-largest by area in the United States, and fourth in the world with 18,076 acres (73.15km2).[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-326) In traffic, DFW airport is the busiest in the state, the fourth busiest in the United States,[&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-327) and sixth worldwide.[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-328) [Southwest Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Airlines "Southwest Airlines"), headquartered in Dallas, has its operations at [Dallas Love Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Love_Field "Dallas Love Field").[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-329) [American Airlines Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines_Group "American Airlines Group")'s [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines "American Airlines") / [American Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Eagle_Airlines "American Eagle Airlines"), the world's largest airline in total passengers-miles transported and passenger fleet size,[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-330) uses DFW as its largest and main [hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_hub "Airline hub").

Texas's second-largest air facility is Houston's [George Bush Intercontinental Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bush_Intercontinental_Airport "George Bush Intercontinental Airport") (IAH). It serves as the largest hub for [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines "United Airlines"), the world's third-largest airline, by passenger-miles flown.[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-331)[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[h\]](#refnote-332) IAH offers service to the most Mexican destinations of any U.S. airport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-333)[&#8202;](#ref-back-334-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-334) The next five largest airports in the state all serve more than three million passengers annually; they include [Austin-Bergstrom International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin-Bergstrom_International_Airport "Austin-Bergstrom International Airport"), [William P. Hobby Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_P._Hobby_Airport "William P. Hobby Airport"), [San Antonio International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_International_Airport "San Antonio International Airport"), [Dallas Love Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Love_Field "Dallas Love Field") and [El Paso International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso_International_Airport "El Paso International Airport"). The smallest airport in the state to be designated an international airport is [Del Rio International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Del_Rio_International_Airport "Del Rio International Airport").

#### Ports

Main article: [List of ports in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_in_the_United_States "List of ports in the United States")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Houston_Ship_Channel.jpg/500px-Houston_Ship_Channel.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Houston_Ship_Channel.jpg) [Port of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Houston "Port of Houston") along the [Houston Ship Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Ship_Channel "Houston Ship Channel")

Around 1,150 [seaports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaports "Seaports") dot Texas's coast with over 1,000 miles (1,600km) of [channels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_(geography) "Channel (geography)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ports1-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-ports1) Ports employ nearly one-million people and handle an average of 317million [metric tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_tons "Metric tons").[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-336) Texas ports connect with the rest of the U.S. Atlantic seaboard with the [Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Intracoastal_Waterway "Gulf Intracoastal Waterway") section of the [Intracoastal Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intracoastal_Waterway "Intracoastal Waterway").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ports1-1)[\[326\]](#refnote-ports1) The [Port of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Houston "Port of Houston") today is the busiest port in the United States in foreign tonnage, second in overall tonnage, and [tenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_world's_busiest_ports_by_cargo_tonnage "List of world's busiest ports by cargo tonnage") worldwide in tonnage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-337) The [Houston Ship Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Ship_Channel "Houston Ship Channel") spans 530 feet (160m) wide by 45 feet (14m) deep by 50 miles (80km) long.[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-338)

#### Railroads

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Westmoreland_Station_August_2019_5.jpg/500px-Westmoreland_Station_August_2019_5.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Westmoreland_Station_August_2019_5.jpg) [DART rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DART_rail "DART rail") in Dallas[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/METRO_Light_Rail3.jpg/500px-METRO_Light_Rail3.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:METRO_Light_Rail3.jpg) [METRORail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/METRORail "METRORail") in Houston

See also: [List of Texas railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_railroads "List of Texas railroads")

Part of the state's [tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboy#Texas_tradition "Cowboy") of cowboys is derived from the massive [cattle drives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_drives_in_the_United_States "Cattle drives in the United States") which its ranchers organized in the nineteenth century to [drive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_drives_in_the_United_States "Cattle drives in the United States") livestock to railroads and markets.

The first railroad to operate in Texas was the [Buffalo Bayou, Brazos and Colorado Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo_Bayou,_Brazos_and_Colorado_Railway "Buffalo Bayou, Brazos and Colorado Railway"), opening in August 1853.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-339) The first railroad to enter Texas from the north, completed in 1872, was the [Missouri–Kansas–Texas Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri%E2%80%93Kansas%E2%80%93Texas_Railroad "Missouri–Kansas–Texas Railroad").[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-340) With increasing railroad access, the ranchers did not have to take their livestock up to the Midwest and shipped beef out from Texas. This caused a decline in the economies of the cow towns.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Since 1911, Texas has led the nation in length of railroad miles within the state. Texas railway length peaked in 1932 at 17,078 miles (27,484km), but declined to 14,006 miles (22,540km) by 2000. While the [Railroad Commission of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_Commission_of_Texas "Railroad Commission of Texas") originally regulated state railroads, in 2005 the state reassigned these duties to TxDOT.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-341)

In the Dallas–Fort Worth area, three public transit agencies provide rail service: [Dallas Area Rapid Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Area_Rapid_Transit "Dallas Area Rapid Transit") (DART), [Denton County Transportation Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denton_County_Transportation_Authority "Denton County Transportation Authority") (DCTA), and [Trinity Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Metro "Trinity Metro").
