DART began operating the first light rail system in the Southwest United States in 1996.[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-342) The [Trinity Railway Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Railway_Express "Trinity Railway Express") (TRE) [commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuter_rail "Commuter rail") service, which connects Fort Worth and Dallas, is provided by [Trinity Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Metro "Trinity Metro") and DART.[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-343) Trinity Metro also operates the [TEXRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TEXRail "TEXRail") commuter rail line, connecting downtown Fort Worth and Northeast Tarrant County to DFW Airport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-344) The [A-train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A-train_(Denton_County) "A-train (Denton County)") commuter rail line, operated by DCTA, acts as an extension of the DART Green line into Denton County.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-345) In the Austin area, [Capital Metropolitan Transportation Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Metropolitan_Transportation_Authority "Capital Metropolitan Transportation Authority") operates a commuter rail service known as [Capital MetroRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_MetroRail "Capital MetroRail") to the northwestern suburbs. The [Metropolitan Transit Authority of Harris County, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transit_Authority_of_Harris_County,_Texas "Metropolitan Transit Authority of Harris County, Texas") (METRO) operates light rail lines called [METRORail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/METRORail "METRORail") in the Houston area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-346)

[Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak "Amtrak") provides Texas with limited intercity passenger rail service. Three scheduled routes serve the state: the daily _[Texas Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Eagle "Texas Eagle")_(Chicago–San Antonio); the tri-weekly _[Sunset Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset_Limited "Sunset Limited")_(New Orleans–Los Angeles), with stops in Texas; and the daily _[Heartland Flyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heartland_Flyer "Heartland Flyer")_(Fort Worth–Oklahoma City). Texas may get one of the nation's first [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail "High-speed rail") line. Plans for a privately funded high-speed rail line between Dallas and Houston have been planned by the [Texas Central Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Central_Railway "Texas Central Railway") company.[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-347)

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Texas "Culture of Texas")

See also: [List of Texas symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_symbols "List of Texas symbols")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Mission_San_Antonio_aka_Alamo.jpg/500px-Mission_San_Antonio_aka_Alamo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mission_San_Antonio_aka_Alamo.jpg) [The Alamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamo_Mission_in_San_Antonio "Alamo Mission in San Antonio") is one of the most recognized symbols of Texas.

Historically, Texas culture comes from a blend of mostly Southern [(Dixie)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_United_States "Southern United States"), Western (frontier), and Southwestern [(Mexican/Anglo fusion)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_United_States "Southwestern United States") influences, varying in degrees of such from one intrastate region to another. A popular food item, the [breakfast burrito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakfast_burrito "Breakfast burrito"), draws from all three, having a soft flour tortilla wrapped around bacon and scrambled eggs or other hot, cooked fillings. Adding to Texas's traditional culture, established in the 18th and 19th centuries, immigration has made Texas a [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot "Melting pot") of cultures from around the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-348)[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-349)

Texas has made a strong mark on national and international pop culture. The entire state is strongly associated with the image of the [cowboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboy "Cowboy") shown in [westerns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westerns "Westerns") and in [country western music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_western_music "Country western music"). The state's numerous oil tycoons are also a popular pop culture topic as seen in the hit TV series _[Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_(TV_series) "Dallas (TV series)")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-350)

The internationally known slogan " [Don't Mess with Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Mess_with_Texas "Don't Mess with Texas")" began as an anti-littering [advertisement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advertising_campaign "Advertising campaign"). Since the campaign's inception in 1986, the phrase has become "an identity statement, a declaration of Texas swagger".[&#8202;](#ref-back-351-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-351)

### Texas self-perception

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Big_Tex.JPG/500px-Big_Tex.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Big_Tex.JPG) [Big Tex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Tex "Big Tex") presided over every [Texas State Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Fair_of_Texas "State Fair of Texas") since 1952 until it was destroyed by a fire in 2012. Since then a new Big Tex was created.

"Texas-sized" describes something that is about the size of Texas,[&#8202;](#ref-back-352-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-352)[&#8202;](#ref-back-353-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-353) or something (usually but not always originating from Texas) that is large compared to other objects of its type.[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-354)[&#8202;](#ref-back-355-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-355)[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-356) Texas was the largest U.S. state until [Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska "Alaska") became a state in 1959. The phrase "everything is bigger in Texas" has been in regular use since at least 1950.[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-357)

### Arts

Further information: [Music of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Texas "Music of Texas")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/ZZ_Top_on_the_Pyramid_Stage_at_Glastonbury_2016_IMG_8527_%2827374417884%29.jpg/500px-ZZ_Top_on_the_Pyramid_Stage_at_Glastonbury_2016_IMG_8527_%2827374417884%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ZZ_Top_on_the_Pyramid_Stage_at_Glastonbury_2016_IMG_8527_(27374417884).jpg) [ZZ Top](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZZ_Top "ZZ Top") performing in 2016. The [power trio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_trio "Power trio") are considered cultural icons of Texas and [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues "Blues")-inspired [rock music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music "Rock music").

Houston is one of only five American cities with permanent professional resident companies in all the major performing arts disciplines: the [Houston Grand Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Grand_Opera "Houston Grand Opera"), the [Houston Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Symphony_Orchestra "Houston Symphony Orchestra"), the [Houston Ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Ballet "Houston Ballet"), and [The Alley Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Alley_Theatre "The Alley Theatre").[&#8202;](#ref-back-houstontheater-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-houstontheater) Known for the vibrancy of its [visual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_arts "Visual arts") and [performing arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts "Performing arts"), the [Houston Theater District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Theater_District "Houston Theater District") ranks second in the country in the number of theater seats in a concentrated downtown area, with 12,948 seats for live performances and 1,480 movie seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-houstontheater-1)[\[349\]](#refnote-houstontheater)
Founded in 1892, [Modern Art Museum of Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Art_Museum_of_Fort_Worth "Modern Art Museum of Fort Worth"), also called "The Modern", is Texas's oldest art museum. Fort Worth also has the [Kimbell Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimbell_Art_Museum "Kimbell Art Museum"), the [Amon Carter Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amon_Carter_Museum "Amon Carter Museum"), the [National Cowgirl Museum and Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cowgirl_Museum_and_Hall_of_Fame "National Cowgirl Museum and Hall of Fame"), the [Will Rogers Memorial Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_Rogers_Memorial_Center "Will Rogers Memorial Center"), and the [Bass Performance Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bass_Performance_Hall "Bass Performance Hall") downtown. The [Arts District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_District,_Dallas "Arts District, Dallas") of [Downtown Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Dallas "Downtown Dallas") has arts venues such as the [Dallas Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Museum_of_Art "Dallas Museum of Art"), the [Morton H. Meyerson Symphony Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morton_H._Meyerson_Symphony_Center "Morton H. Meyerson Symphony Center"), [the Margot and Bill Winspear Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Margot_and_Bill_Winspear_Opera_House "The Margot and Bill Winspear Opera House"), [the Trammell & Margaret Crow Collection of Asian Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Trammell_&_Margaret_Crow_Collection_of_Asian_Art "The Trammell & Margaret Crow Collection of Asian Art"), and the [Nasher Sculpture Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasher_Sculpture_Center "Nasher Sculpture Center").[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-359)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Houston_Symphony.jpg/500px-Houston_Symphony.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Houston_Symphony.jpg) [Houston Symphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Symphony "Houston Symphony") at the [Jones Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Hall "Jones Hall")

The [Deep Ellum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_Ellum "Deep Ellum") district within Dallas became popular during the 1920s and 1930s as the prime [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz "Jazz") and [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues "Blues") hotspot in the Southern United States. The name Deep Ellum comes from local people pronouncing "Deep Elm" as "Deep Ellum".[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-360) Artists such as [Blind Lemon Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Lemon_Jefferson "Blind Lemon Jefferson"), [Robert Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Johnson_(musician) "Robert Johnson (musician)"), Huddie " [Lead Belly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_Belly "Lead Belly")" Ledbetter, and [Bessie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Smith "Bessie Smith") played in early Deep Ellum clubs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-361-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-361)

Austin, _[The Live Music Capital of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Austin "Music of Austin")_, boasts "more live music venues per capita than such music hotbeds as Nashville, Memphis, Los Angeles, Las Vegas or New York City".[&#8202;](#ref-back-362-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-362) The city's music revolves around the [nightclubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightclub "Nightclub") on [6th Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_Street_(Austin) "6th Street (Austin)"); events like the film, music, and [multimedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multimedia "Multimedia") festival [South by Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_by_Southwest "South by Southwest"); the longest-running concert music program on American television, _[Austin City Limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_City_Limits "Austin City Limits")_; and the [Austin City Limits Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_City_Limits_Music_Festival "Austin City Limits Music Festival") held in [Zilker Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zilker_Park "Zilker Park").[&#8202;](#ref-back-363-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-363)

Since 1980, San Antonio has evolved into "The [Tejano Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tejano_Music "Tejano Music") Capital Of The World".[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-364) The [Tejano Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tejano_Music_Awards "Tejano Music Awards") have provided a forum to create greater awareness and appreciation for Tejano music and culture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-365-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-365)

### Sports

Main article: [Sports in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Texas "Sports in Texas")

Further information: [List of University Interscholastic League events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_University_Interscholastic_League_events "List of University Interscholastic League events")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Cowboys_Stadium_full_view.jpg/500px-Cowboys_Stadium_full_view.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cowboys_Stadium_full_view.jpg) [AT&T Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT&T_Stadium "AT&T Stadium"), home of the [Dallas Cowboys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Cowboys "Dallas Cowboys"), who are commonly known as [America's Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America's_Team "America's Team")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/2014_NBA_Playoffs_Dallas_Mavericks_vs._San_Antonio_Spurs.jpg/500px-2014_NBA_Playoffs_Dallas_Mavericks_vs._San_Antonio_Spurs.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2014_NBA_Playoffs_Dallas_Mavericks_vs._San_Antonio_Spurs.jpg) Playoff game between the [San Antonio Spurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Spurs "San Antonio Spurs") and the [Dallas Mavericks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Mavericks "Dallas Mavericks") in [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_NBA_playoffs "2014 NBA playoffs"); the Spurs won the [NBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_NBA_Finals "2014 NBA Finals") that year.[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Houston_Astros_vs._Texas_Rangers_July_2019_19.jpg/500px-Houston_Astros_vs._Texas_Rangers_July_2019_19.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Houston_Astros_vs._Texas_Rangers_July_2019_19.jpg) Texan baseball fans attending a game between the state's two [MLB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB "MLB") teams ( [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_(baseball) "Texas Rangers (baseball)") and [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros "Houston Astros")) at [Globe Life Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globe_Life_Field "Globe Life Field") in [Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Texas "Arlington, Texas")

Within the ["Big Four" professional leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada "Major professional sports leagues in the United States and Canada"), Texas has two [NFL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League "National Football League") teams (the [Dallas Cowboys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Cowboys "Dallas Cowboys") and the [Houston Texans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Texans "Houston Texans")), two [MLB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball "Major League Baseball") teams (the [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros "Houston Astros") and the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_(baseball) "Texas Rangers (baseball)")),[&#8202;](#ref-back-366-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-366)[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-367) three [NBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Basketball_Association "National Basketball Association") teams (the [San Antonio Spurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Spurs "San Antonio Spurs"), the [Houston Rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Rockets "Houston Rockets"), and the [Dallas Mavericks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Mavericks "Dallas Mavericks")), and one [NHL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Hockey_League "National Hockey League") team (the [Dallas Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Stars "Dallas Stars")). The [Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth_metroplex "Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex") is one of only [twelve American metropolitan areas that host sports teams from all the "Big Four" professional leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._cities_with_teams_from_four_major_league_sports "U.S. cities with teams from four major league sports"). Outside of the "Big Four", Texas also has a [WNBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_National_Basketball_Association "Women's National Basketball Association") team (the [Dallas Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Wings "Dallas Wings")), three [Major League Soccer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Soccer "Major League Soccer") teams ( [Austin FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_FC "Austin FC"), [Houston Dynamo FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Dynamo_FC "Houston Dynamo FC") and [FC Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Dallas "FC Dallas")), and one [NWSL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Soccer_League "National Women's Soccer League") team (the [Houston Dash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Dash "Houston Dash")).

[Collegiate athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_athletics "College athletics") have deep significance in Texas culture, especially [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_football "American football"). The state has twelve [Division I-FBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_I-FBS "Division I-FBS") schools, the most in the nation. Four of the state's schools claim at least one national championship in football: the Texas Longhorns, the Texas A&M Aggies, the TCU Horned Frogs, and the SMU Mustangs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-368-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-368)[&#8202;](#ref-back-369-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-369)[&#8202;](#ref-back-370-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-370)[&#8202;](#ref-back-371-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-371) According to a survey of Division I-A coaches, the [rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_rivalry "College rivalry") between the [University of Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Oklahoma "University of Oklahoma") and the University of Texas at Austin, the [Red River Shootout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_River_Shootout "Red River Shootout"), ranks the third-best in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-372-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-372) The TCU Horned Frogs and SMU Mustangs also share a rivalry and compete annually in the [Battle for the Iron Skillet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_for_the_Iron_Skillet "Battle for the Iron Skillet"). A fierce rivalry, the [Lone Star Showdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lone_Star_Showdown "Lone Star Showdown"), also exists between the state's two largest universities, Texas A&M University and the University of Texas at Austin. The athletics portion of the Lone Star Showdown rivalry was paused between 2012, when Texas A&M left the Big 12 and joined the SEC, and 2024, when Texas joined them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-373-0)[\[364\]](#refnote-373)

The [University Interscholastic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Interscholastic_League "University Interscholastic League") (UIL) organizes most primary and secondary school competitions. Events organized by UIL include contests in athletics (the most popular being [high school football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_school_football "High school football")) as well as artistic and academic subjects.[&#8202;](#ref-back-374-0)[\[365\]](#refnote-374)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Rodeo_competition_at_the_Star_of_Texas_Fair_and_Rodeo%2C_produced_by_Rodeo_Austin_in_Austin%2C_Texas_LCCN2015630182.tif/lossy-page1-500px-Rodeo_competition_at_the_Star_of_Texas_Fair_and_Rodeo%2C_produced_by_Rodeo_Austin_in_Austin%2C_Texas_LCCN2015630182.tif.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rodeo_competition_at_the_Star_of_Texas_Fair_and_Rodeo,_produced_by_Rodeo_Austin_in_Austin,_Texas_LCCN2015630182.tif) [Rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodeo "Rodeo") (the state sport) in [Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin,_Texas "Austin, Texas")

Texans also enjoy [rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodeo "Rodeo"). The world's first rodeo was hosted in [Pecos, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pecos,_Texas "Pecos, Texas").[&#8202;](#ref-back-375-0)[\[366\]](#refnote-375) The annual [Houston Livestock Show and Rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Livestock_Show_and_Rodeo "Houston Livestock Show and Rodeo") is the largest rodeo in the world. The [Southwestern Exposition and Livestock Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Exposition_and_Livestock_Show "Southwestern Exposition and Livestock Show") in Fort Worth is the oldest continuously running rodeo incorporating many of the state's most historic traditions into its annual events. Dallas hosts the [State Fair of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Fair_of_Texas "State Fair of Texas") each year at [Fair Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Park "Fair Park").[&#8202;](#ref-back-376-0)[\[367\]](#refnote-376)

[Texas Motor Speedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Motor_Speedway "Texas Motor Speedway") hosts annual [NASCAR Cup Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASCAR_Cup_Series "NASCAR Cup Series") and [IndyCar Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IndyCar_Series "IndyCar Series") auto races since 1997. Since 2012, Austin's [Circuit of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_of_the_Americas "Circuit of the Americas") plays host to a round of the [Formula 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_1 "Formula 1") World Championship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-377-0)[\[368\]](#refnote-377)

The [Panther City Lacrosse Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panther_City_Lacrosse_Club "Panther City Lacrosse Club") is a professional lacrosse team in the [National Lacrosse League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Lacrosse_League "National Lacrosse League"). They have played local matches at Dickies Arena in Fort Worth, Texas since their inaugural 2021–2022 season.[&#8202;](#ref-back-378-0)[\[369\]](#refnote-378)

## Education

Main article: [Education in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Texas "Education in Texas")

The second [president of the Republic of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_the_Republic_of_Texas "List of Presidents of the Republic of Texas"), [Mirabeau B. Lamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirabeau_B._Lamar "Mirabeau B. Lamar"), is known as the _Father of Texas Education_. During his term, the state set aside three [leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_(unit) "League (unit)") in each county for public schools. An additional 50 leagues of land set aside for the support of two universities would later become the basis of the state's [Permanent University Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_University_Fund "Permanent University Fund").[&#8202;](#ref-back-379-0)[\[370\]](#refnote-379) Lamar's actions set the foundation for a Texas-wide public school system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-380-0)[\[371\]](#refnote-380)

Texas has [over 1,000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_school_districts_in_Texas "List of school districts in Texas") [school districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district "School district"), all administered by the [Texas Education Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Education_Agency "Texas Education Agency"), and all districts except the [Stafford Municipal School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stafford_Municipal_School_District "Stafford Municipal School District") are independent from municipal government and many cross city boundaries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-381-0)[\[372\]](#refnote-381) School districts have the power to [tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation "Taxation") their residents and to assert [eminent domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminent_domain "Eminent domain") over privately owned property. Due to court-mandated equitable school financing, the state has a tax redistribution system called the " [Robin Hood plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Hood_plan "Robin Hood plan")" which transfers property tax revenue from wealthy school districts to poor ones.[&#8202;](#ref-back-382-0)[\[373\]](#refnote-382) The TEA has no authority over private or [homeschooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeschooling "Homeschooling") activities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-383-0)[\[374\]](#refnote-383)

Students in Texas take the [State of Texas Assessments of Academic Readiness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STAAR "STAAR") (STAAR) in primary and secondary school. STAAR assess students' attainment of [reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading_(activity) "Reading (activity)"), writing, [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics "Mathematics"), science, and [social studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_studies "Social studies") skills required under Texas education standards and the [No Child Left Behind Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act "No Child Left Behind Act"). The test replaced the [Texas Assessment of Knowledge and Skills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Assessment_of_Knowledge_and_Skills "Texas Assessment of Knowledge and Skills") (TAKS) test in the 2011–2012 school year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-384-0)[\[375\]](#refnote-384)

Generally prohibited in the [Western world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world"), [school corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_corporal_punishment "School corporal punishment") is not unusual in the more conservative, rural areas of the state,[&#8202;](#ref-back-385-0)[\[376\]](#refnote-385)[&#8202;](#ref-back-386-0)[\[377\]](#refnote-386)[&#8202;](#ref-back-387-0)[\[378\]](#refnote-387) with 28,569 public school students [paddled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddle_(spanking) "Paddle (spanking)") at least one time,[&#8202;](#ref-back-388-0)[\[i\]](#refnote-388) according to government data for the 2011–2012 school year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL-0)[\[379\]](#refnote-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL) The rate of school corporal punishment in Texas is surpassed only by [Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi "Mississippi"), [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama "Alabama"), and [Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas "Arkansas").[&#8202;](#ref-back-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL-1)[\[379\]](#refnote-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL)

### Higher education

Further information: [List of colleges and universities in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Texas "List of colleges and universities in Texas")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/University_of_Texas_at_Austin_August_2019_30_%28Littlefield_Fountain_and_Main_Building%29.jpg/500px-University_of_Texas_at_Austin_August_2019_30_%28Littlefield_Fountain_and_Main_Building%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:University_of_Texas_at_Austin_August_2019_30_(Littlefield_Fountain_and_Main_Building).jpg) The [University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_Austin "University of Texas at Austin")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Roy_Gustav_Cullen_Building.JPG/500px-Roy_Gustav_Cullen_Building.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Roy_Gustav_Cullen_Building.JPG) [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston "University of Houston")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/TAMUcampus.jpg/500px-TAMUcampus.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TAMUcampus.jpg) [Texas A&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A&M_University "Texas A&M University")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Lovett_Hall.jpg/500px-Lovett_Hall.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lovett_Hall.jpg) [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University "Rice University")

The state's two most widely recognized flagship universities are [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Austin "The University of Texas at Austin") and [Texas A&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A&M_University "Texas A&M University"), ranked as the 21st[&#8202;](#ref-back-390-0)[\[380\]](#refnote-390) and 41st[&#8202;](#ref-back-391-0)[\[381\]](#refnote-391) best universities in the nation according to 2020's latest Center for World University Rankings report, respectively. Some observers[&#8202;](#ref-back-392-0)[\[382\]](#refnote-392) also include the [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston "University of Houston") and [Texas Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University "Texas Tech University") as tier one flagships alongside UT Austin and A&M.[&#8202;](#ref-back-393-0)[\[383\]](#refnote-393)[&#8202;](#ref-back-394-0)[\[384\]](#refnote-394) The [Texas Higher Education Coordinating Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Higher_Education_Coordinating_Board "Texas Higher Education Coordinating Board") ranks the state's public universities into three distinct tiers:[&#8202;](#ref-back-UT_System_Tier_One-0)[\[385\]](#refnote-UT_System_Tier_One)

- National Research Universities (Tier 1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-396-0)[\[386\]](#refnote-396)[&#8202;](#ref-back-397-0)[\[387\]](#refnote-397)
  - [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Austin "The University of Texas at Austin")
  - [Texas A&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A&M_University "Texas A&M University")
  - [Texas Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University "Texas Tech University")
  - [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston "University of Houston")
  - [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University "Rice University")
  - [The University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_Health_Science_Center_at_Houston "The University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston")
  - [The University of Texas at Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Arlington "The University of Texas at Arlington")
  - [The University of Texas at Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Dallas "The University of Texas at Dallas")
  - [The University of North Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_North_Texas "The University of North Texas")
  - [The University of Texas at El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_El_Paso "The University of Texas at El Paso")
- Emerging Research Universities (Tier 2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-UT_System_Tier_One-1)[\[385\]](#refnote-UT_System_Tier_One)
  - [The University of Texas at San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_San_Antonio "The University of Texas at San Antonio")
  - [Texas State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_University "Texas State University")
- Comprehensive Universities (Tier 3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-UT_System_Tier_One-2)[\[385\]](#refnote-UT_System_Tier_One)
  - All other public universities (25 in total)

The Carnegie Foundation classifies six of Texas's universities as Tier One research institutions:[&#8202;](#ref-back-398-0)[\[388\]](#refnote-398)

- [The University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_Health_Science_Center_at_Houston "The University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston")
- [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University "Rice University")
- [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_Austin "University of Texas at Austin")
- [Texas A&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A&M_University "Texas A&M University")
- [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston "University of Houston")
- [Texas Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University "Texas Tech University")

Texas's alternative affirmative action plan, [Texas House Bill 588](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_House_Bill_588 "Texas House Bill 588"), guarantees Texas students who graduated in the top 10 percent of their high school class automatic admission to state-funded universities. This does not apply to [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Austin "The University of Texas at Austin"), which automatically admits Texas students who graduated in the top 6 percent of their high school class.[&#8202;](#ref-back-399-0)[\[389\]](#refnote-399) The bill encourages demographic diversity while attempting to avoid problems stemming from the _[Hopwood v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopwood_v._Texas "Hopwood v. Texas")_ (1996) case.[&#8202;](#ref-back-400-0)[\[390\]](#refnote-400)

Thirty-six public universities exist in Texas, of which 32 belong to one of the six state university systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-401-0)[\[391\]](#refnote-401)[&#8202;](#ref-back-402-0)[\[392\]](#refnote-402) Discovery of minerals on [Permanent University Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_University_Fund "Permanent University Fund") land, particularly oil, has helped fund the rapid growth of the state's two largest university systems: the [University of Texas System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_System "University of Texas System") and the [Texas A&M System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A&M_University_System "Texas A&M University System"). The four other university systems: the [University of Houston System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston_System "University of Houston System"), the [University of North Texas System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_North_Texas_System "University of North Texas System"), the [Texas State System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_University_System "Texas State University System"), and the [Texas Tech System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University_System "Texas Tech University System") are not funded by the Permanent University Fund.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Smyrl-0)[\[393\]](#refnote-Smyrl)

The University of Texas at Austin and Texas A&M University are the flagship universities of the [University of Texas System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_System "University of Texas System") and [Texas A&M University System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A&M_University_System "Texas A&M University System"), respectively. Both were established by the Texas Constitution and hold stakes in the Permanent University Fund.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Smyrl-1)[\[393\]](#refnote-Smyrl)

The state has sought to expand the number of flagship universities by elevating some of its seven institutions designated as "emerging research universities". The two expected to emerge first are the University of Houston and Texas Tech University, likely in that order according to discussions on the House floor of the 82nd Texas Legislature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-404-0)[\[394\]](#refnote-404)

The state is home to various private institutions of higher learning—ranging from liberal arts colleges to a nationally recognized top-tier research university. [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University "Rice University") in Houston is one of the leading teaching and research universities of the United States and is ranked the nation's 17th-best overall university by _U.S. News & World Report_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-405-0)[\[395\]](#refnote-405)

[Trinity University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_University_(Texas) "Trinity University (Texas)"), a private, primarily undergraduate liberal arts university in San Antonio, has ranked first among universities granting primarily bachelor's and select master's degrees in the Western United States for 20 consecutive years by _U.S. News_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-406-0)[\[396\]](#refnote-406) Private universities include [Abilene Christian University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abilene_Christian_University "Abilene Christian University"), [Austin College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_College "Austin College"), [Baylor University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baylor_University "Baylor University"), [University of Mary Hardin–Baylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Mary_Hardin%E2%80%93Baylor "University of Mary Hardin–Baylor"), and [Southwestern University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_University "Southwestern University").[&#8202;](#ref-back-407-0)[\[397\]](#refnote-407)[&#8202;](#ref-back-408-0)[\[398\]](#refnote-408)[&#8202;](#ref-back-409-0)[\[399\]](#refnote-409)

Universities in Texas host three presidential libraries: [George Bush Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bush_Presidential_Library "George Bush Presidential Library") at Texas A&M University,[&#8202;](#ref-back-410-0)[\[400\]](#refnote-410) the [Lyndon Baines Johnson Library and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_Baines_Johnson_Library_and_Museum "Lyndon Baines Johnson Library and Museum") at The University of Texas at Austin,[&#8202;](#ref-back-411-0)[\[401\]](#refnote-411)[&#8202;](#ref-back-412-0)[\[402\]](#refnote-412) and the [George W. Bush Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Presidential_Library "George W. Bush Presidential Library") at [Southern Methodist University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Methodist_University "Southern Methodist University").[&#8202;](#ref-back-413-0)[\[403\]](#refnote-413)

## Healthcare

Main article: [Healthcare in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Texas "Healthcare in Texas")

See also: [List of hospitals in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Texas "List of hospitals in Texas")

Notwithstanding the concentration of elite medical centers in the state, [The Commonwealth Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Commonwealth_Fund "The Commonwealth Fund") ranks the Texas healthcare system the third worst in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-insurancenet-0)[\[404\]](#refnote-insurancenet) Texas ranks close to last in access to healthcare, quality of care, avoidable hospital spending, and equity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-insurancenet-1)[\[404\]](#refnote-insurancenet) In May 2006, Texas initiated the program "code red" in response to the report the state had 25.1 percent of the population without health insurance, the largest proportion in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-415-0)[\[405\]](#refnote-415)

The [Trust for America's Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trust_for_America's_Health "Trust for America's Health") ranked Texas 15th highest in adult [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity "Obesity"): 27.2 percent of the state's population is obese.[&#8202;](#ref-back-416-0)[\[406\]](#refnote-416) The 2008 [Men's Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men's_Health_(magazine) "Men's Health (magazine)") obesity survey ranked four Texas cities among the top 25 fattest cities in America: Houston ranked 6th, Dallas 7th, El Paso 8th, and [Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Texas "Arlington, Texas") 14th.[&#8202;](#ref-back-obese2-0)[\[407\]](#refnote-obese2) Texas had only one city (Austin, ranked 21st) in the top 25 "fittest cities" in America.[&#8202;](#ref-back-obese2-1)[\[407\]](#refnote-obese2) The state is ranked forty-second in the percentage of residents who engage in regular exercise according to a 2007 study.[&#8202;](#ref-back-418-0)[\[408\]](#refnote-418)

Texas has the [highest maternal mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_Healthcare_System_in_Texas "Maternal Healthcare System in Texas") in the developed world, and the rate by which Texas women died from pregnancy-related complications doubled from 2010 to 2014, to 23.8 per 100,000—a rate unmatched in any other U.S. state or economically developed country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-419-0)[\[409\]](#refnote-419) In May 2021, the state legislature passed the [Texas Heartbeat Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Heartbeat_Act "Texas Heartbeat Act"), which banned [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion "Abortion") from as early as six weeks of pregnancy, except to save the life of the mother. The Act allows private citizens to sue abortion providers and anyone else who assists in an abortion, except for the woman on whom the abortion is performed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-420-0)[\[410\]](#refnote-420)[&#8202;](#ref-back-421-0)[\[411\]](#refnote-421) On August 25, 2022, another law took effect that made committing abortion at any stage of pregnancy a felony punishable by life in prison.[&#8202;](#ref-back-422-0)[\[412\]](#refnote-422)

Access to [allergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allergy "Allergy") and [immunology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunology "Immunology") specialists in Texas is unevenly distributed. While the state is served by approximately 462 allergists and immunologists, these specialists are primarily concentrated in major urban centers such as Dallas, Houston, and Austin. In rural areas of Texas, the average availability is significantly lower, with approximately one specialist for every 50,000 residents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-423-0)[\[413\]](#refnote-423)

### Medical research

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Texas_medical_center_sundown.jpg/500px-Texas_medical_center_sundown.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Texas_medical_center_sundown.jpg) The [Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Medical_Center "Texas Medical Center") in Houston

Texas has many elite research medical centers. The state has 15 [medical schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Texas#Health_science "List of colleges and universities in Texas"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-424-0)[\[414\]](#refnote-424) four dental schools,[&#8202;](#ref-back-425-0)[\[415\]](#refnote-425) and two [optometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optometry "Optometry") schools.[&#8202;](#ref-back-426-0)[\[416\]](#refnote-426) Texas has two [Biosafety Level 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosafety_Level_4 "Biosafety Level 4") (BSL-4) laboratories: one at [The University of Texas Medical Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_Medical_Branch "The University of Texas Medical Branch") (UTMB) in Galveston,[&#8202;](#ref-back-427-0)[\[417\]](#refnote-427) and the other at the [Southwest Foundation for Biomedical Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Foundation_for_Biomedical_Research "Southwest Foundation for Biomedical Research") in San Antonio—the first privately owned BSL-4 lab in the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-428-0)[\[418\]](#refnote-428)

The [Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Medical_Center "Texas Medical Center") in Houston holds the world's largest concentration of research and healthcare institutions, with over 50 member institutions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-429-0)[\[419\]](#refnote-429) Texas Medical Center performs the most heart transplants in the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-430-0)[\[420\]](#refnote-430) The [University of Texas M. D. Anderson Cancer Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_M._D._Anderson_Cancer_Center "University of Texas M. D. Anderson Cancer Center") in Houston is a highly regarded academic institution that centers around cancer patient care, research, education and prevention.[&#8202;](#ref-back-431-0)[\[421\]](#refnote-431)

San Antonio's [South Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Texas_Medical_Center "South Texas Medical Center") facilities rank sixth in clinical medicine research impact in the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-432-0)[\[422\]](#refnote-432) The [University of Texas Health Science Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTHSCSA "UTHSCSA") is another highly ranked research and educational institution in San Antonio.[&#8202;](#ref-back-433-0)[\[423\]](#refnote-433)[&#8202;](#ref-back-434-0)[\[424\]](#refnote-434)

Both the [American Heart Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Heart_Association "American Heart Association") and the [University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_Southwestern_Medical_Center "University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center") call Dallas home.[&#8202;](#ref-back-UTSWAboutUs-0)[\[425\]](#refnote-UTSWAboutUs) The [institution's medical school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_Southwestern_Medical_School "University of Texas Southwestern Medical School") employs the most medical school [Nobel laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_laureates "Nobel laureates") in the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-UTSWAboutUs-1)[\[425\]](#refnote-UTSWAboutUs)[&#8202;](#ref-back-436-0)[\[426\]](#refnote-436)

## Government and politics

The current [Texas Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Constitution "Texas Constitution") was adopted in 1876. Like many [states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_constitution_(United_States) "State constitution (United States)"), it explicitly provides for a separation of powers. The state's Bill of Rights is much larger than its [federal counterpart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights "United States Bill of Rights"), and has provisions unique to Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-437-0)[\[427\]](#refnote-437)

### State government

Main article: [Government of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Texas "Government of Texas")

Further information: [Law of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Texas "Law of Texas")

See also: [List of Texas state agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_state_agencies "List of Texas state agencies")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Texas_State_Capitol_Night.jpg/960px-Texas_State_Capitol_Night.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Texas_State_Capitol_Night.jpg) The [Texas State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_Capitol "Texas State Capitol") at night

Texas has a plural [executive branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_branch "Executive branch") system limiting the power of the governor, which is a weak executive compared to some other states. Except for the [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Texas "Secretary of State of Texas"), voters elect executive officers independently; candidates are directly answerable to the public, not the governor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pluralexec-0)[\[428\]](#refnote-pluralexec) This election system has led to some executive branches split between parties and reduced the ability of the governor to carry out a program. When [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") president [George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush "George W. Bush") served as Texas's governor, the state had a [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States) "Democratic Party (United States)") lieutenant governor, [Bob Bullock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Bullock "Bob Bullock"). The executive branch positions consist of the [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Texas "Governor of Texas"), [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Texas "Lieutenant Governor of Texas"), comptroller of public accounts, land commissioner, [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Attorney_General "Texas Attorney General"), agriculture commissioner, the three-member [Texas Railroad Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Railroad_Commission "Texas Railroad Commission"), the State Board of Education, and the secretary of state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pluralexec-1)[\[428\]](#refnote-pluralexec)

The [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism "Bicameralism") [Texas Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Legislature "Texas Legislature") consists of the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_House_of_Representatives "Texas House of Representatives"), with 150 members, and a [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Senate "Texas Senate"), with 31 members. The [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Texas_House_of_Representatives "Speaker of the Texas House of Representatives") leads the House, and the lieutenant governor, the Senate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-439-0)[\[429\]](#refnote-439) The Legislature meets in regular session biennially for just over a hundred days, but the governor can call for special sessions as often as desired (notably, the Legislature cannot call itself into session).[&#8202;](#ref-back-440-0)[\[430\]](#refnote-440) The state's [fiscal year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_year "Fiscal year") begins September1.[&#8202;](#ref-back-441-0)[\[431\]](#refnote-441)

The [judiciary of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_Texas "Judiciary of Texas") is among the most complex in the United States, with many layers and overlapping jurisdictions. Texas has two courts of last resort: the [Texas Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Supreme_Court "Texas Supreme Court"), for civil cases, and the [Texas Court of Criminal Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Court_of_Criminal_Appeals "Texas Court of Criminal Appeals"). Except for some municipal benches, partisan elections select judges at all levels of the judiciary; the governor fills vacancies by appointment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-442-0)[\[432\]](#refnote-442) Texas is notable for [its use of capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Texas "Capital punishment in Texas"), having led the country in executions since capital punishment was reinstated in the _[Gregg v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregg_v._Georgia "Gregg v. Georgia")_ case.[&#8202;](#ref-back-443-0)[\[433\]](#refnote-443)

The [Texas Ranger Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Ranger_Division "Texas Ranger Division") of the [Texas Department of Public Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Department_of_Public_Safety "Texas Department of Public Safety") is a [law enforcement agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police "Police") with statewide [jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction "Jurisdiction"). Over the years, the Texas Rangers have investigated crimes ranging from [murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_in_Texas_law "Murder in Texas law") to political corruption. They have acted as [riot police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riot_police "Riot police") and as detectives, protected the Texas governor, tracked down fugitives, and functioned as a paramilitary force. The Texas Rangers were unofficially created by [Stephen F. Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_F._Austin "Stephen F. Austin") in 1823 and formally constituted in 1835. The Rangers were integral to several important events of Texas history and some of the best-known criminal cases in the history of the [Old West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Old_West "American Old West").[&#8202;](#ref-back-444-0)[\[434\]](#refnote-444)

The Texas constitution defines the responsibilities of county governments, which serve as agents of the state. Commissioners court and court judges are elected to serve as the administrative arm. Most cities in the state, those over 5,000 in population, have [home-rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home-rule "Home-rule") governments. The vast majority of these have charters for [council-manager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council-manager "Council-manager") forms of government, by which voters elect council members, who hire a professional city manager as an operating officer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-445-0)[\[435\]](#refnote-445)

### Politics

Main article: [Politics of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Texas "Politics of Texas")

Further information: [Political party strength in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_Texas "Political party strength in Texas")

See also: [Republican Party of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_Texas "Republican Party of Texas") and [Texas Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Democratic_Party "Texas Democratic Party")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/37_Lyndon_Johnson_3x4.jpg/500px-37_Lyndon_Johnson_3x4.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:37_Lyndon_Johnson_3x4.jpg) [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson "Lyndon B. Johnson") of Texas, 36th president of the United States[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/George-W-Bush.jpeg/500px-George-W-Bush.jpeg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George-W-Bush.jpeg) [George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush "George W. Bush") of Texas, 43rd president of the United States

Texas has historically supported the [Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") in U.S. and statewide elections since the late 20th century. Republicans won every statewide election from 1994 to 2026,[&#8202;](#ref-back-TexasGOPStreak-0)[\[436\]](#refnote-TexasGOPStreak) and the last time Texas voted for a Democratic presidential candidate was for [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter "Jimmy Carter") in [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_United_States_presidential_election "1976 United States presidential election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fox4TexasStreak-0)[\[437\]](#refnote-Fox4TexasStreak)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Elliott2026-1)[\[140\]](#refnote-Elliott2026)

The [Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Democratic_Party "Texas Democratic Party") dominated [Texas politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Texas "Politics of Texas") from the turn of the 20th century, imposing racial segregation and white supremacy. It held power until after passage in the mid-1960s of national civil rights legislation enforcing constitutional rights of all citizens.[&#8202;](#ref-back-448-0)[\[438\]](#refnote-448)[&#8202;](#ref-back-449-0)[\[439\]](#refnote-449) The party returned to power in the state after the [2026 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_elections "2026 United States elections"), when Democrats won all statewide elections, ending Republicans' 32-year electoral winning streak in the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Elliott2026-2)[\[140\]](#refnote-Elliott2026)

The state's conservative White voters began to support Republican presidential candidates by the mid-20th century. After this period, they supported Republicans for local and state offices as well, and most Whites became Republican Party members.[&#8202;](#ref-back-450-0)[\[440\]](#refnote-450) The party also attracted some minorities, but many have continued to vote for Democratic candidates. The shift to the Republican Party is much-attributed to the fact the Democratic Party became increasingly [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States "Liberalism in the United States") during the 20th century, and thus increasingly out-of-touch with the average Texas voter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-HistoryTXvoters-0)[\[441\]](#refnote-HistoryTXvoters) As Texas was always a [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States") state, voters switched to the Republicans, which now more closely reflected their beliefs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-HistoryTXvoters-1)[\[441\]](#refnote-HistoryTXvoters)[&#8202;](#ref-back-PBSredstate-0)[\[442\]](#refnote-PBSredstate) Commentators have also attributed the shift to Republican political consultant [Karl Rove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rove "Karl Rove"), who managed numerous political campaigns in Texas in the 1980s and 1990s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PBSredstate-1)[\[442\]](#refnote-PBSredstate) Other stated reasons included court-ordered redistricting and the growing Sun Belt cities' conservatism unlike the liberal tradition of their northeastern counterparts as well as the popularity of Republican party in the suburbs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Calvert-6)[\[120\]](#refnote-Calvert)

The [2003 Texas redistricting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Texas_redistricting "2003 Texas redistricting") of Congressional districts led by Republican [Tom DeLay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_DeLay "Tom DeLay"), was called by _The New York Times_ "an extreme case of partisan [gerrymandering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrymandering "Gerrymandering")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYTGerry-0)[\[443\]](#refnote-NYTGerry) A group of Democratic legislators, the "Texas Eleven", fled the state in a [quorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quorum "Quorum")-busting effort to prevent the legislature from acting, but was unsuccessful.[&#8202;](#ref-back-454-0)[\[444\]](#refnote-454) The state had already redistricted following the 2000 census. Despite these efforts, the legislature passed a map heavily in favor of Republicans, based on 2000 data and ignoring the estimated nearly one million new residents in the state since then. Career attorneys and analysts at the Department of Justice objected to the plan as diluting the votes of African American and Hispanic voters, but political appointees overrode them and approved it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYTGerry-1)[\[443\]](#refnote-NYTGerry) Legal challenges to the redistricting reached the national [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States "Supreme Court of the United States") in the case _[League of United Latin American Citizens v. Perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_United_Latin_American_Citizens_v._Perry "League of United Latin American Citizens v. Perry")_ (2006), but the court ruled in favor of the state (and Republicans).[&#8202;](#ref-back-455-0)[\[445\]](#refnote-455)

In the [2014 Texas elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_elections,_2014 "Texas elections, 2014"), the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement "Tea Party movement") made large gains, with numerous Tea Party favorites being elected into office, including [Dan Patrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Patrick_(politician) "Dan Patrick (politician)") as lieutenant governor,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fernandez-0)[\[446\]](#refnote-Fernandez)[&#8202;](#ref-back-457-0)[\[447\]](#refnote-457) [Ken Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Paxton "Ken Paxton") as attorney general,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fernandez-1)[\[446\]](#refnote-Fernandez)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grissom-0)[\[448\]](#refnote-Grissom) in addition to numerous other candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-Grissom-1)[\[448\]](#refnote-Grissom) including conservative Republican [Greg Abbott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Abbott "Greg Abbott") as governor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-459-0)[\[449\]](#refnote-459)

Texas voters lean toward [fiscal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism "Fiscal conservatism"), while enjoying the benefits of significant federal investment in the state in military and other facilities achieved by the power of the Solid South in the 20th century. They also tend to have [socially conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism "Social conservatism") values.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TPoliticalCulture1-1)[\[260\]](#refnote-TPoliticalCulture1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-460-0)[\[450\]](#refnote-460)

Since 1980, most Texas voters have supported Republican presidential candidates. Austin, Dallas, Houston, San Antonio, and El Paso consistently lean Democratic in both local and statewide elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-City_Journal-1)[\[122\]](#refnote-City_Journal) Residents of counties along the [Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande "Rio Grande") closer to the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border "Mexico–United States border"), where there are many Latino residents, generally vote for Democratic Party candidates, while most other rural and suburban areas of Texas have shifted to voting for Republican Party candidates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-461-0)[\[451\]](#refnote-461)[&#8202;](#ref-back-462-0)[\[452\]](#refnote-462)

In the [119th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/119th_United_States_Congress "119th United States Congress"), 25 of the 38 [congressional districts in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas's_congressional_districts "Texas's congressional districts") are held by Republicans and the other 13 by Democrats. Texas's Senators are [John Cornyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Cornyn "John Cornyn") and [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz "Ted Cruz"). Between 1988 and 2026, Texans did not elect any Democrats to the [United States Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate"). This pattern was disrupted in 2026 with the election of Democrat [James Talarico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Talarico "James Talarico"). Prior to Talarico's victory, the last Democrat to represent Texas in the US Senate was [Lloyd Bentsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Bentsen "Lloyd Bentsen").

The state's Democratic voters are made up primarily by liberal and [minority groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_groups "Minority groups") in Austin, Beaumont, Dallas, El Paso, Houston, and San Antonio as well as minority voters in East and South Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-463-0)[\[453\]](#refnote-463)[&#8202;](#ref-back-464-0)[\[454\]](#refnote-464)[&#8202;](#ref-back-465-0)[\[455\]](#refnote-465)
According to a study by the [Cato Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cato_Institute "Cato Institute"), Texas ranks last in personal freedom among the states, by factors including incarceration rates, cannabis laws, civil asset forfeiture policies, educational freedom, gambling laws, marriage freedom, and travel freedom.[&#8202;](#ref-back-466-0)[\[456\]](#refnote-466)[&#8202;](#ref-back-467-0)[\[457\]](#refnote-467)

Lesser parties that have ballot access are the [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Texas "Green Party of Texas") and the [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_(United_States) "Libertarian Party (United States)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-468-0)[\[458\]](#refnote-468)

### Criminal law

Main article: [Crime in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Texas "Crime in Texas")

See also: [List of law enforcement agencies in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_law_enforcement_agencies_in_Texas "List of law enforcement agencies in Texas")

Texas has a reputation for very harsh penalties for criminal offenses. It is one of the 32 states that practice [capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Texas "Capital punishment in Texas"), and since the [US Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Supreme_Court "US Supreme Court") allowed capital punishment to resume in 1976, 40% of all U.S. executions have taken place in Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-469-0)[\[459\]](#refnote-469) In 2018, Texas had the [8th highest incarceration rate in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_incarceration_and_correctional_supervision_rate "List of U.S. states and territories by incarceration and correctional supervision rate")[&#8202;](#ref-back-470-0)[\[460\]](#refnote-470) Texas also has strong [right of self-defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_self-defense "Right of self-defense") and [self defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-defense "Self-defense") laws, allowing citizens to use lethal force to defend themselves, their families, or their property.[&#8202;](#ref-back-471-0)[\[461\]](#refnote-471) Texas has one of the strictest [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Texas "Abortion in Texas") bans in the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-472-0)[\[462\]](#refnote-472)

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-213)[↑](#ref-back-213-0)Mean of density in 1mi radii circles around each person

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-3)[↑](#ref-back-3-0)Elevation adjusted to [North American Vertical Datum of 1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Vertical_Datum_of_1988 "North American Vertical Datum of 1988")
2. [&#8202;](#refnote-9)[↑](#ref-back-9-0)In [Peninsular Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Spanish "Peninsular Spanish"), the spelling variant _Tejas_ is also used alongside _Texas_. According to the _[Diccionario panhispánico de dudas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diccionario_panhisp%C3%A1nico_de_dudas "Diccionario panhispánico de dudas")_ by the [Royal Spanish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Spanish_Academy "Royal Spanish Academy") and the [Association of Academies of the Spanish Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Academies_of_the_Spanish_Language "Association of Academies of the Spanish Language"), the spelling version with ⟨j⟩ aligns with modern-day orthographic conventions and is correct; however, the spelling with ⟨x⟩ is recommended, as it is the one that is used in [Mexican Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Spanish "Mexican Spanish").[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-8) See [Spanish orthography §History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_orthography#History "Spanish orthography").
3. [&#8202;](#refnote-10)[↑](#ref-back-10-0)locally also [/ˈtɛksɪz/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [_TEK-siz_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key "Help:Pronunciation respelling key");[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-7) [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language "Spanish language"): _Texas_ or _Tejas_[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-9)pronounced[\[ˈtexas\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Spanish "Help:IPA/Spanish")[Play audio](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/3d/Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg/Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg "File:Pronunciation of Texas in Spanish.ogg")
4. [&#8202;](#refnote-44)[↑](#ref-back-44-0)Cabeza de Vaca wrote, "They went about with a firebrand, setting fire to the plains and timber so as to drive off the mosquitos, and also to get lizards and similar things which they eat, to come out of the soil. In the same manner they kill deer, encircling them with fires, and they do it also to deprive the animals of pasture, compelling them to go for food where the Indians want."[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-43)
5. [&#8202;](#refnote-47)[↑](#ref-back-47-0)Vázquez de Coronado wrote, "Two kinds of people travel around these plains with the cows; one is called [Querechos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Querecho_Indians "Querecho Indians") and the others [Teyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teyas_Indians "Teyas Indians"); they are very well built, and painted, and are enemies of each other. They have no other settlement or location than comes from traveling around with the cows. They kill all of these they wish and tan the hides, with which they clothe themselves and make their tents, and they eat the flesh, sometimes even raw, and they also even drink the blood when thirsty. The tents they make are like field tents, and they set them up over poles they have made for this purpose, which come together and are tied at the top, and when they go from one place to another they carry them on some dogs they have, of which they have many, and they load them with the tents and poles and other things, for the country is so level, as I said, that they can make use of these, because they carry the poles dragging along on the ground. The sun is what they worship most."[&#8202;](#ref-back-journeycoronado-1)[\[42\]](#refnote-journeycoronado)
6. [&#8202;](#refnote-219)[↑](#ref-back-219-0)Persons of Hispanic or Latino origin are not distinguished between total and partial ancestry.
7. [&#8202;](#refnote-264)[↑](#ref-back-264-0)Second to [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California")
8. [&#8202;](#refnote-332)[↑](#ref-back-332-0)Based on the industry-standard measure of revenue passenger-kilometers/miles flown
9. [&#8202;](#refnote-388)[↑](#ref-back-388-0)This figure refers to only the number of students paddled, regardless of whether a student was spanked multiple times in a year, and does not refer to the number of instances of corporal punishment, which would be substantially higher.

["Between the Creeks"](https://texashistory.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metapth752794/m2/1/high_res_d/Between%20_the_Creeks_by_Gwen_Pettit_compiled_by_Melinda_Fisher.pdf)(PDF). _texashistory.unt.edu_. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200320204123/https://texashistory.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metapth752794/m2/1/high_res_d/Between%20_the_Creeks_by_Gwen_Pettit_compiled_by_Melinda_Fisher.pdf)(PDF) from the original on March 20, 2020. Retrieved December 28, 2020. The Southern states, especially the hills of Tennessee and Alabama, were impoverished; war tensions still split neighborhoods. Soon, empty houses had crude signs that stated that the former inhabitants had "Gone to Texas." Church records, also, had the phrase, "Gone to Texas" by numerous names on their roles. So many families left Maury County, Tenn., to settle in eastern Collin County, just across East Fork, that several communities, such as Culleoka, have names directly from south Maury County. That group joined relatives that had come here in the 1850s. Most new immigrants had some link to Collin County, which brought them here. They stayed with relatives and friends until they could find a place to settle. Landowners recruited farmers from the old states by persuading relatives and former neighbors to come. However, numerous families, in the pioneer tradition, loaded their children and belongings in a wagon and headed toward the unknown west. Clarksville, Bonham and Dallas newspapers reported how many wagons passed through each day and how many were camped on the "jockey yards" waiting to find a place to settle. Some of these had sold farms and had money to buy land, but most of these immigrants became tenant farmers and worked on shares.

[Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200929111744/https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/lumber-industry) from the original on September 29, 2020. Retrieved December 28, 2020. But relatively, the industry's status is far below its dominant position at the beginning of the twentieth century. Then it was the state's largest manufacturing enterprise, first among Texas industries in generating income, and the largest employer of labor in the Lone Star State.

113. [↑](#ref-back-118-0)McElroy, Wendy (April 1, 2002). ["World War I and the Suppression of Dissent"](https://www.independent.org/article/2002/04/01/world-war-i-and-the-suppression-of-dissent/). _Independent Institute_. Retrieved May 26, 2025.
114. [↑](#ref-back-119-0)["'War against war': Americans for peace in World War I – National Constitution Center"](https://constitutioncenter.org/blog/war-against-war-americans-for-peace-in-world-war-i). _National Constitution Center – constitutioncenter.org_. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201112022807/https://constitutioncenter.org/blog/war-against-war-americans-for-peace-in-world-war-i) from the original on November 12, 2020. Retrieved December 28, 2020. Morris Hillquit sought to keep alive the ties of his Socialist Party to its comrades abroad. Senator Robert La Follette filled many a speech with praise for progressives in other countries who shared his hatred for militarism. Henry Ford chartered an ocean liner to transport himself and dozens of other activists across the Atlantic, where they lobbied neutral governments to embrace a peace plan they would press on the warring powers. These Americans, like most critics of the war elsewhere in the world, wanted to create a new global order based on cooperative relationships between nation states and their gradual disarmament. Militarism, they argued, isolated peoples behind walls of mutual fear and loathing. Until April 1917, this formidable coalition of idealists—or realists—did much to keep the nation at peace. They may even have had a majority of Americans on their side until just weeks before Congress, at Wilson's behest, voted to declare war. To prevent that from happening, peace activists pressed for a national referendum on the question, confident that "the people" would recoil from fighting and paying the bills in order to help one group of European powers conquer another.
